English for Work · Health & life sciences

Oncology English

Specialist English for diagnosis discussions, staging, biomarkers, treatment intent, toxicity reports, scan reviews, research consent, and supportive care.

8 practical lessonsIntermediate to advanced1 complete learner book
Start practicing →

Words into action

Read the situation.
Find your words.
Make yourself clear.

For oncologists, medical oncologists, radiation oncologists, surgical oncologists, oncology fellows.

Open learner book (PDF) ↗

Copy complete AI prompts ↓

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Deliver an established finding in small steps while responding to emotion and information preferences.
  • Explain closely related oncology terms without converting one into another.
  • Explain the difference between a molecular finding, a relevant option, and treatment eligibility.
  • Explain treatment goals and tradeoffs without promising a response or presenting consent as a formality.
  • Give a concise urgent handoff that distinguishes a reported symptom from an established treatment complication.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Prompts

All people, records, figures, and encounters are fictional. This is professional English practice, not medical advice, clinical training, a treatment protocol, or certification. Use current evidence, authorized supervision where required, local law, and your actual clinical and emergency procedures. Do not use an exercise to decide that symptoms can safely wait, select treatment, or calculate a dose. Communication about privacy, consent, and professional duties depends on jurisdiction and setting. Use qualified language support and protect real patient information.

01Explaining a new cancer diagnosisThe first clear sentence+

Your goal

Deliver an established finding in small steps while responding to emotion and information preferences.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

The first clear sentence

Dr Imani meets Maya after a pathology report confirms a malignant tumor. Staging is incomplete, and no treatment recommendation or prognosis estimate has been established. Maya wants the main finding first and then a pause. Maya has not yet decided whether a relative should join. The clinician must give the finding plainly, acknowledge the response, and separate the diagnosis from unanswered questions.

Dr Imani: Oncologist / Maya: Patient

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is established?

Answer and explanation

D. A malignant tumor on pathology

The pathology confirms malignancy; staging and individual outcomes remain unresolved.

2. What does Maya request?

Answer and explanation

A. The main finding followed by a pause

Maya's stated preference is a clear main finding and time to respond.

3. What family decision is unfinished?

Answer and explanation

C. Whether a relative should join

Maya has not yet chosen whether to include a relative.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The diagnosis is established in this case, but stage, treatment, and prognosis are not. Do not soften a confirmed finding into vagueness or turn it into an unsupported prediction.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
pathology report
Laboratory interpretation of examined tissue or cells.review the pathology report
biopsy
Removal of tissue or cells for examination.explain the biopsy finding
malignancy
Cancerous disease characterized by malignant cells or tissue.explain a confirmed malignancy
histology
Microscopic study of tissue structure and features.review the histology
primary tumor
Tumor at the site where the cancer began.identify the primary tumor
diagnostic disclosure
Communication of an established diagnosis to the patient.prepare for diagnostic disclosure
warning statement
Brief introduction preparing a listener for difficult information.use a clear warning statement
information preference
How much detail a person wants and how they want it presented.check the information preference
emotional acknowledgment
Recognition of the listener's expressed feelings or response.offer emotional acknowledgment
prognostic uncertainty
Incomplete ability to predict an individual's future disease course.explain prognostic uncertainty
staging workup
Investigations used to establish the extent of cancer.complete the staging workup
multidisciplinary team
Professionals from different specialties contributing to a care discussion.consult the multidisciplinary team
second opinion
Assessment or advice from another qualified clinician.arrange a second opinion
care navigator
Professional helping a person move through services and appointments.contact the care navigator
support person
Individual the patient wants involved for assistance.invite a chosen support person
diagnostic certainty
Degree to which the diagnosis is established by evidence.distinguish diagnostic certainty
information overload
More information than a listener can usefully process at one time.avoid information overload
follow-up consultation
Later meeting to review findings, options, or unresolved questions.arrange a follow-up consultation
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Ask permission before involving a relative. A support person is not automatically a substitute decision-maker.

C Notice the language

A pause is part of the conversation

Silence after difficult news need not be filled with statistics. Check what the person wants next and who they want involved, without assuming either preference.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Prepare for the finding
I am afraid the result is not what we hoped for.
Name the diagnosis
The tissue examination confirms a malignant tumor.
Allow a pause
We can stop here for a moment.
Acknowledge emotion
I can see that this is a great deal to take in.
Separate questions
We know the diagnosis; we do not yet know the full extent.
Ask about support
Would you like someone you choose to join us?
Limit a prediction
I cannot give an individual outlook from this finding alone.
Offer continuity
We will arrange another conversation when the remaining results are available.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Confirms
Appropriate here because the fictional pathology finding is established, unlike the incomplete stage.
Not yet
Keeps an unanswered question visible without treating uncertainty as a negative result.
Would you like
Invites a preference rather than assuming how much information or family involvement is wanted.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which opening is clearest?

Answer and explanation

C. The tissue examination confirms a malignant tumor.

Naming the confirmed tumor states the established finding without obscuring it or inventing later conclusions.

2. Which response respects Maya's preference?

Answer and explanation

C. Pause after the main finding.

Maya explicitly requests time to respond before receiving more detail.

3. Which conclusion exceeds the facts?

Answer and explanation

B. The diagnosis alone establishes Maya's individual prognosis.

A confirmed diagnosis does not supply the missing stage or an individual outlook.

4. What should precede family involvement?

Answer and explanation

D. Clarifying Maya's wishes and the appropriate permissions

Family involvement must reflect the patient's wishes and the applicable information-sharing rules.

E Conversations

The first clear sentence

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Dr Imani: Oncologist / Maya: Patient

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • information overload
  • prognostic uncertainty
  • second opinion
  • staging workup
  • information preference
  • malignant tumor
  • pathology report
  • biopsy
  • care navigator
  • support person
  1. Dr Imani
    Before we discuss the report, how much detail would you like in this first conversation?
  2. Maya
    The main finding first, please. My 1 may change once I have heard it.
  3. Dr Imani
    I am afraid the result is not what we hoped for. The tissue examination confirms a 2.
  4. Maya
    Please give me a moment. I knew the 3 might find something, but hearing that is different.
  5. Dr Imani
    Of course. We can pause. I can see this is a great deal to take in.
  6. Maya
    When I am ready, could you explain what the 4 tells you and what it does not?
  7. Dr Imani
    It establishes the cancer diagnosis. It does not yet give us every answer about the extent or treatment.
  8. Maya
    So the 5 is a separate part of understanding what happens next?
  9. Dr Imani
    Yes. We need those remaining details before completing the treatment discussion or estimating your individual outlook.
  10. Maya
    I was about to ask how long I have. I hear that there is still 6.
  11. Dr Imani
    I will answer as honestly as I can, including what we do not know. I will not invent a number.
  12. Maya
    Thank you. I might want my brother here, but I have not chosen a 7 for this conversation yet.
  13. Dr Imani
    You can decide. We will discuss what you want shared rather than assume that every family member is involved.
  14. Maya
    Could someone help me understand the appointments? I am finding even ordinary arrangements difficult to absorb.
  15. Dr Imani
    Our 8 can help with those arrangements while we keep the clinical questions with the appropriate team.
  16. Maya
    I do not want a large pack of unexplained information to be my only connection with the team.
  17. Dr Imani
    That would risk 9. We will give manageable information and a clear contact route.
  18. Maya
    I may also want another clinician to review the findings once I understand the next steps.
  19. Dr Imani
    We can discuss a 10 and arrange a follow-up conversation. You do not need every decision settled in this first exchange.
  20. Maya
    For now, I understand the diagnosis is confirmed and the full extent is not. Please let us stop there briefly.

Answers and explanations
  1. information preference Turn 2. Information preference describes Maya's requested amount and sequence of detail for this difficult conversation.
  2. malignant tumor Turn 3. A malignant tumor is the cancerous finding established by the pathology in this fictional case.
  3. biopsy Turn 4. A biopsy provides tissue or cells for examination; here its report has established a malignant finding.
  4. pathology report Turn 6. The pathology report contains the interpretation of the examined tissue and supports the established diagnosis.
  5. staging workup Turn 8. The staging workup establishes the extent of disease and is still incomplete in this case.
  6. prognostic uncertainty Turn 10. Prognostic uncertainty means the individual future course cannot be established from the available finding alone.
  7. support person Turn 12. A support person is someone Maya chooses to involve; a relative is not automatically entitled to the information.
  8. care navigator Turn 15. A care navigator helps coordinate access and appointments without replacing the clinician's diagnostic or treatment responsibilities.
  9. information overload Turn 17. Information overload occurs when the volume of detail exceeds what a person can usefully process at the time.
  10. second opinion Turn 19. A second opinion is another qualified assessment and can be discussed without treating the request as disloyalty.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Dr Imani: Before we discuss the report, how much detail would you like in this first conversation?
  2. Maya: The main finding first, please. My information preference may change once I have heard it.
  3. Dr Imani: I am afraid the result is not what we hoped for. The tissue examination confirms a malignant tumor.
  4. Maya: Please give me a moment. I knew the biopsy might find something, but hearing that is different.
  5. Dr Imani: Of course. We can pause. I can see this is a great deal to take in.
  6. Maya: When I am ready, could you explain what the pathology report tells you and what it does not?
  7. Dr Imani: It establishes the cancer diagnosis. It does not yet give us every answer about the extent or treatment.
  8. Maya: So the staging workup is a separate part of understanding what happens next?
  9. Dr Imani: Yes. We need those remaining details before completing the treatment discussion or estimating your individual outlook.
  10. Maya: I was about to ask how long I have. I hear that there is still prognostic uncertainty.
  11. Dr Imani: I will answer as honestly as I can, including what we do not know. I will not invent a number.
  12. Maya: Thank you. I might want my brother here, but I have not chosen a support person for this conversation yet.
  13. Dr Imani: You can decide. We will discuss what you want shared rather than assume that every family member is involved.
  14. Maya: Could someone help me understand the appointments? I am finding even ordinary arrangements difficult to absorb.
  15. Dr Imani: Our care navigator can help with those arrangements while we keep the clinical questions with the appropriate team.
  16. Maya: I do not want a large pack of unexplained information to be my only connection with the team.
  17. Dr Imani: That would risk information overload. We will give manageable information and a clear contact route.
  18. Maya: I may also want another clinician to review the findings once I understand the next steps.
  19. Dr Imani: We can discuss a second opinion and arrange a follow-up conversation. You do not need every decision settled in this first exchange.
  20. Maya: For now, I understand the diagnosis is confirmed and the full extent is not. Please let us stop there briefly.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A diagnosis before the staging discussion

A patient separates what is confirmed from what is pending.

  1. Patient: I heard the word malignant, but I could not take in the rest of the explanation.
  2. Oncologist: The tissue assessment confirms cancer. We can pause before discussing what is still being investigated.
  3. Patient: Does that confirmation also tell you exactly where it has spread?
  4. Oncologist: No. Diagnosis and staging answer different questions, and the stage is not established here.
  5. Patient: I would like the confirmed finding repeated in plain language before hearing more terminology.
  6. Oncologist: Of course. We should not soften the meaning so much that the finding becomes unclear.
  7. Patient: Could my chosen support person join the next part with my permission?
  8. Oncologist: Yes. We can confirm what you want shared and whom you want present.
  9. Patient: Then I can separate the diagnosis from questions that the further assessment still needs to answer.
  10. Oncologist: That distinction will remain clear in the summary and the next discussion.
The pathology wording is unfamiliar

A clinician explains the role of a report without inventing its details.

  1. Oncologist: Which part of the pathology report would you like explained before we move on?
  2. Patient: I recognize the diagnosis line, but the descriptive terms make me think every word predicts my future.
  3. Oncologist: The report describes the tissue findings; not every term is a prognosis.
  4. Patient: Could we distinguish the type of tumor from how the overall situation is staged?
  5. Oncologist: Yes. Those concepts should be explained separately using the actual report and other assessment.
  6. Patient: I also want to know which questions the tissue sample cannot answer by itself.
  7. Oncologist: That limitation matters, and we should not imply that one sample settles every clinical question.
  8. Patient: Please avoid giving a survival estimate simply because I ask what the words mean.
  9. Oncologist: We will distinguish terminology, established findings, and any appropriate individualized prognosis discussion.
  10. Patient: Then the report can become understandable without turning each unfamiliar word into a prediction.
Permission to include a relative

A patient clarifies who may hear difficult news.

  1. Patient: My brother came with me, but I want to hear the diagnosis privately first.
  2. Oncologist: We can arrange that and discuss what you would like shared afterward.
  3. Patient: He usually handles appointments, so people sometimes assume he should answer every question.
  4. Oncologist: Helping with appointments does not automatically make him the recipient of every clinical detail.
  5. Patient: I may want him present when we discuss the next investigation.
  6. Oncologist: We can revisit your preference and confirm the appropriate permission for that discussion.
  7. Patient: I also want the record to show that I am making my own decisions.
  8. Oncologist: The actual decision-making arrangements should be clear rather than inferred from who accompanies you.
  9. Patient: Thank you. Support is helpful, but I do not want my voice replaced.
  10. Oncologist: We can include the support you choose while keeping the explanation and questions directed to you.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The first clear sentence
  1. Read the corrected disclosure slowly. Pause after the finding and after Maya's request for time.
  2. Swap roles. Repeat the distinction between a confirmed diagnosis and an incomplete stage without adding a prognosis.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Check who should join

Alex wants partner Ren to attend the next results conversation but has not authorized disclosure to other relatives. The next meeting will review outstanding findings; no outcome is supplied.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Alex: "I would like ___ to join."

Answer and explanation

B. Ren

Ren is the chosen partner for the next conversation in this scenario.

2. Clinician: "The permission does not automatically include other ___."

Answer and explanation

A. relatives

The specified preference does not authorize broader family disclosure by itself.

3. Alex: "Some findings remain ___."

Answer and explanation

A. outstanding

The next meeting is intended to review findings that are still outstanding.

4. Clinician: "No outcome has been ___."

Answer and explanation

D. supplied

The case provides an arrangement for discussion, not a new clinical result.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

Extend the vocabularyPractice the grammarRehearse with AI

Copy a finished AI prompt for this lesson

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Explaining a new cancer diagnosis
Original fictional case: Dr Imani meets Maya after a pathology report confirms a malignant tumor. Staging is incomplete, and no treatment recommendation or prognosis estimate has been established. Maya wants the main finding first and then a pause. Maya has not yet decided whether a relative should join. The clinician must give the finding plainly, acknowledge the response, and separate the diagnosis from unanswered questions.
Language workshop: The first clear sentence
Communication goal: Deliver an established finding in small steps while responding to emotion and information preferences.
Language guidance: The diagnosis is established in this case, but stage, treatment, and prognosis are not. Do not soften a confirmed finding into vagueness or turn it into an unsupported prediction.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I am afraid the result is not what we hoped for. / The tissue examination confirms a malignant tumor. / We can stop here for a moment. / I can see that this is a great deal to take in. / We know the diagnosis; we do not yet know the full extent. / Would you like someone you choose to join us? / I cannot give an individual outlook from this finding alone. / We will arrange another conversation when the remaining results are available. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: pathology report: Laboratory interpretation of examined tissue or cells. / biopsy: Removal of tissue or cells for examination. / malignancy: Cancerous disease characterized by malignant cells or tissue. / histology: Microscopic study of tissue structure and features. / primary tumor: Tumor at the site where the cancer began. / diagnostic disclosure: Communication of an established diagnosis to the patient. / warning statement: Brief introduction preparing a listener for difficult information. / information preference: How much detail a person wants and how they want it presented. / emotional acknowledgment: Recognition of the listener's expressed feelings or response. / prognostic uncertainty: Incomplete ability to predict an individual's future disease course. / staging workup: Investigations used to establish the extent of cancer. / multidisciplinary team: Professionals from different specialties contributing to a care discussion. / second opinion: Assessment or advice from another qualified clinician. / care navigator: Professional helping a person move through services and appointments. / support person: Individual the patient wants involved for assistance. / diagnostic certainty: Degree to which the diagnosis is established by evidence. / information overload: More information than a listener can usefully process at one time. / follow-up consultation: Later meeting to review findings, options, or unresolved questions. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected disclosure slowly. Pause after the finding and after Maya's request for time. Swap roles. Repeat the distinction between a confirmed diagnosis and an incomplete stage without adding a prognosis. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Dr Imani: Oncologist / Maya: Patient
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Alex wants partner Ren to attend the next results conversation but has not authorized disclosure to other relatives. The next meeting will review outstanding findings; no outcome is supplied.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Dr Imani: That would risk information overload. We will give manageable information and a clear contact route.
Maya: I may also want another clinician to review the findings once I understand the next steps.
Dr Imani: We can discuss a second opinion and arrange a follow-up conversation. You do not need every decision settled in this first exchange.
Maya: For now, I understand the diagnosis is confirmed and the full extent is not. Please let us stop there briefly.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Vocabulary: pathology report: Laboratory interpretation of examined tissue or cells. / biopsy: Removal of tissue or cells for examination. / malignancy: Cancerous disease characterized by malignant cells or tissue. / histology: Microscopic study of tissue structure and features. / primary tumor: Tumor at the site where the cancer began. / diagnostic disclosure: Communication of an established diagnosis to the patient. / warning statement: Brief introduction preparing a listener for difficult information. / information preference: How much detail a person wants and how they want it presented. / emotional acknowledgment: Recognition of the listener's expressed feelings or response. / prognostic uncertainty: Incomplete ability to predict an individual's future disease course. / staging workup: Investigations used to establish the extent of cancer. / multidisciplinary team: Professionals from different specialties contributing to a care discussion. / second opinion: Assessment or advice from another qualified clinician. / care navigator: Professional helping a person move through services and appointments. / support person: Individual the patient wants involved for assistance. / diagnostic certainty: Degree to which the diagnosis is established by evidence. / information overload: More information than a listener can usefully process at one time. / follow-up consultation: Later meeting to review findings, options, or unresolved questions. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

02Separating stage, grade, and prognosisTwo terms on one report+

Your goal

Explain closely related oncology terms without converting one into another.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

Two terms on one report

Theo has a pathology report that includes a tumor grade. Imaging needed for staging is still pending. Dr Malik must explain that grade concerns microscopic features while stage describes extent using the applicable cancer-specific system. No grade value, stage, or individual survival estimate is supplied. Theo has mistaken the grade for a completed stage.

Theo: Patient / Dr Malik: Oncologist

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which item is available?

Answer and explanation

D. A pathology report containing grade

The pathology grade is available while the staging workup is unfinished.

2. What is pending?

Answer and explanation

D. Imaging needed for staging

The brief explicitly identifies staging imaging as still pending.

3. What has Theo confused?

Answer and explanation

B. Grade with stage

Theo has interpreted the grade as though it were the completed stage.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Grade and stage are not synonyms. Staging systems differ by cancer type; do not assign a TNM category or stage from a vocabulary definition.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
tumor grade
Classification of microscopic tumor features under the relevant grading system.explain tumor grade
cancer stage
Description of cancer extent under the applicable staging system.establish the cancer stage
differentiation
Degree to which tumor cells resemble normal cells of their origin.describe cellular differentiation
local extent
Spread of a tumor within its original area.assess local extent
regional lymph node
Lymph node in the region draining or associated with the tumor site.assess regional lymph nodes
distant metastasis
Cancer spread to a site distant from the primary tumor.evaluate distant metastasis
clinical stage
Stage based on clinical evaluation before definitive pathological staging when applicable.record the clinical stage
pathological stage
Stage incorporating surgical and pathological findings where applicable.distinguish pathological stage
TNM classification
Cancer-specific classification describing tumor, regional nodes, and distant metastasis.explain the TNM classification
stage group
Overall stage category combining applicable staging information.confirm the stage group
staging investigation
Test used to help determine cancer extent.arrange a staging investigation
imaging finding
Observation reported from a diagnostic image.review an imaging finding
indeterminate finding
Finding whose nature or significance is not yet established.explain an indeterminate finding
nodal involvement
Presence of cancer in lymph nodes.assess nodal involvement
localized disease
Cancer confined to a defined local site in the relevant context.describe localized disease
metastatic disease
Cancer that has spread to distant body sites.discuss metastatic disease
prognostic factor
Feature associated with a disease's expected course or outcome.interpret a prognostic factor
staging completion
Point at which the necessary staging information has been assembled.confirm staging completion
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

An indeterminate imaging finding is not automatically metastatic disease. Preserve the report's uncertainty and the need for clinical interpretation.

C Notice the language

Correct the interpretation, not the person

Technical terms can appear together on reports while answering different questions. Use a clear contrast and a brief check-back rather than telling the patient they read it incorrectly.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Introduce the contrast
These two terms answer different questions.
Explain grade
Grade describes microscopic features of the tumor.
Explain stage
Stage describes the extent using the system for this cancer.
Name the missing evidence
The required imaging is still pending.
Avoid a shortcut
We cannot convert the grade into a stage.
Preserve uncertainty
Indeterminate does not mean confirmed spread.
Limit prognosis
A stage label is not an exact prediction for one person.
Check the contrast
Which term describes extent, and which describes the tissue features?
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Describes versus predicts
A classification describes evidence; it does not predict an individual's exact outcome.
Still pending
States that an investigation is unfinished rather than negative.
Not automatically
Blocks an invalid inference while leaving room for appropriate assessment.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which contrast is correct?

Answer and explanation

C. Grade concerns microscopic features; stage concerns extent.

The two classifications address different properties and cannot replace each other.

2. What does pending imaging mean?

Answer and explanation

A. The required result is not yet available.

Pending describes availability and does not establish the result's clinical meaning.

3. Which wording preserves an indeterminate finding?

Answer and explanation

B. Its significance is not yet established.

Indeterminate means uncertainty remains and should not be converted into certainty.

4. Which claim is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

B. The grade supplies Theo's exact survival time.

Neither the grade nor the supplied facts establish an individual survival prediction.

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Theo: Patient / Dr Malik: Oncologist

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • cancer stage
  • indeterminate finding
  • Tumor grade
  • staging completion
  • Differentiation
  • local extent
  • TNM classification
  • Distant metastasis
  • prognostic factor
  • regional lymph node
  1. Theo
    My report gives a grade. I thought that meant you already knew the stage of the cancer.
  2. Dr Malik
    1 describes microscopic features. Stage answers a different question about the extent of disease.
  3. Theo
    Then I may have combined two separate pieces of information when I explained the report at home.
  4. Dr Malik
    That is understandable. The 2 is not complete while we are waiting for the required imaging.
  5. Theo
    I also saw a word about how much the cells resemble ordinary tissue. Where does that fit?
  6. Dr Malik
    3 concerns that resemblance. It can inform grading, but it does not by itself tell us where disease is present.
  7. Theo
    What does the staging investigation need to establish beyond what the microscope has shown?
  8. Dr Malik
    Depending on the cancer, it examines features such as 4, nearby nodes, and possible disease elsewhere.
  9. Theo
    So a nearby lymph node and a finding in a distant organ would not be described identically?
  10. Dr Malik
    Correct. A 5 and a distant site have different roles in the applicable staging system.
  11. Theo
    I have heard people use the word metastatic for any unusual spot. Is that too definite?
  12. Dr Malik
    Yes. An 6 has not yet been characterized. We must not relabel uncertainty as confirmed spread.
  13. Theo
    That distinction helps. I would rather the report's uncertainty stay visible than be translated into the worst possibility.
  14. Dr Malik
    Exactly. 7 is a specific conclusion, not a synonym for every finding that needs further review.
  15. Theo
    Will every cancer use the same letters and numbers once those investigations are complete?
  16. Dr Malik
    No. 8 applies in cancer-specific ways, and some cancers use other systems.
  17. Theo
    And even when the stage is known, it will not tell you exactly what happens to me personally?
  18. Dr Malik
    Correct. A 9 informs a discussion; it is not an exact individual timetable.
  19. Theo
    Then I can say the grade is reported, but the stage still needs the remaining investigation results.
  20. Dr Malik
    Yes. We will confirm 10 and explain the conclusion when the evidence is available.

Answers and explanations
  1. Tumor grade Turn 2. Tumor grade describes microscopic features under the relevant system and is distinct from the cancer's extent.
  2. cancer stage Turn 4. Cancer stage describes extent under the applicable system and remains incomplete while necessary evidence is pending.
  3. Differentiation Turn 6. Differentiation concerns how closely tumor cells resemble normal cells and can contribute to grading in the relevant system.
  4. local extent Turn 8. Local extent describes disease in the original area and is one component rather than the entirety of staging.
  5. regional lymph node Turn 10. A regional lymph node belongs to the relevant nearby drainage region and differs from a distant disease site.
  6. indeterminate finding Turn 12. An indeterminate finding has unresolved nature or significance and is not the same as confirmed cancer spread.
  7. Distant metastasis Turn 14. Distant metastasis means established cancer spread to a distant site, which an uncertain spot does not prove.
  8. TNM classification Turn 16. TNM classifies tumor, regional nodes, and metastasis in cancer-specific ways and is not a universal staging shortcut.
  9. prognostic factor Turn 18. A prognostic factor is associated with the expected course but does not determine one person's exact future.
  10. staging completion Turn 20. Staging completion means the necessary information has been assembled and interpreted rather than assumed from grade alone.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Theo: My report gives a grade. I thought that meant you already knew the stage of the cancer.
  2. Dr Malik: Tumor grade describes microscopic features. Stage answers a different question about the extent of disease.
  3. Theo: Then I may have combined two separate pieces of information when I explained the report at home.
  4. Dr Malik: That is understandable. The cancer stage is not complete while we are waiting for the required imaging.
  5. Theo: I also saw a word about how much the cells resemble ordinary tissue. Where does that fit?
  6. Dr Malik: Differentiation concerns that resemblance. It can inform grading, but it does not by itself tell us where disease is present.
  7. Theo: What does the staging investigation need to establish beyond what the microscope has shown?
  8. Dr Malik: Depending on the cancer, it examines features such as local extent, nearby nodes, and possible disease elsewhere.
  9. Theo: So a nearby lymph node and a finding in a distant organ would not be described identically?
  10. Dr Malik: Correct. A regional lymph node and a distant site have different roles in the applicable staging system.
  11. Theo: I have heard people use the word metastatic for any unusual spot. Is that too definite?
  12. Dr Malik: Yes. An indeterminate finding has not yet been characterized. We must not relabel uncertainty as confirmed spread.
  13. Theo: That distinction helps. I would rather the report's uncertainty stay visible than be translated into the worst possibility.
  14. Dr Malik: Exactly. Distant metastasis is a specific conclusion, not a synonym for every finding that needs further review.
  15. Theo: Will every cancer use the same letters and numbers once those investigations are complete?
  16. Dr Malik: No. TNM classification applies in cancer-specific ways, and some cancers use other systems.
  17. Theo: And even when the stage is known, it will not tell you exactly what happens to me personally?
  18. Dr Malik: Correct. A prognostic factor informs a discussion; it is not an exact individual timetable.
  19. Theo: Then I can say the grade is reported, but the stage still needs the remaining investigation results.
  20. Dr Malik: Yes. We will confirm staging completion and explain the conclusion when the evidence is available.
Dialogue in the book, page 19 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Grade is not stage

A patient confuses two classification terms.

  1. Patient: The report gives a grade, so I assumed that number was also my stage.
  2. Oncologist: Grade and stage describe different features and should not be treated as interchangeable.
  3. Patient: Can you explain the distinction without assigning a stage before the remaining assessment?
  4. Oncologist: Grade concerns features of the tumor cells or tissue; stage concerns the extent of disease.
  5. Patient: Then a grade alone cannot tell me where disease is present in the body.
  6. Oncologist: Correct. The actual staging process uses the relevant clinical and investigation findings.
  7. Patient: I do not want a number copied from one category into the other.
  8. Oncologist: We should name each category and preserve what remains unestablished.
  9. Patient: Could the summary state clearly that the stage is still pending?
  10. Oncologist: Yes. A clear record should not turn an available grade into an invented stage.
An imaging finding needs correlation

Two clinicians discuss a finding that may affect staging.

  1. Radiologist: There is a finding that needs correlation before its significance is described as established.
  2. Oncologist: Thank you. I will not automatically label it metastatic disease in the patient summary.
  3. Radiologist: Please retain the report's uncertainty and the actual recommendation for further review.
  4. Oncologist: We need to explain what is seen separately from what it may mean.
  5. Radiologist: An earlier comparison study may be useful, if the relevant images can be obtained.
  6. Oncologist: I will clarify whether those images are available rather than assume the comparison is complete.
  7. Radiologist: The current report should not be shortened to a definitive stage without the required assessment.
  8. Oncologist: Agreed. The staging discussion must use the actual combined evidence.
  9. Radiologist: Please let the patient know which question remains unresolved and who will address it.
  10. Oncologist: We will provide a concrete next step without promising the conclusion of the review.
A stage question is not a prognosis timetable

A patient asks what classification can predict.

  1. Patient: If you eventually establish the stage, will that tell me exactly how long I will live?
  2. Oncologist: Stage provides important information, but it is not an individual timetable.
  3. Patient: I want honest information without having a population statistic presented as my guaranteed outcome.
  4. Oncologist: That distinction matters. Any prognosis discussion needs the actual diagnosis, context, and uncertainty explained.
  5. Patient: Would treatment response and other individual factors also belong in that discussion?
  6. Oncologist: Yes, where relevant, rather than reducing the whole situation to one classification.
  7. Patient: I am not asking you to avoid the subject, only to describe what the information supports.
  8. Oncologist: We can discuss it directly while making the limits of prediction clear.
  9. Patient: Could we first confirm which findings are established and which tests remain outstanding?
  10. Oncologist: We should. Prognostic language must not rest on a stage that has not yet been established.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Two terms on one report
  1. Read the corrected terminology discussion. Contrast grade, stage, and an indeterminate finding.
  2. Swap roles. Repeat the final summary without supplying a stage or prognosis that the case does not contain.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Keep an uncertain scan finding uncertain

A report calls a lung finding indeterminate. The oncology team is reviewing it. No metastatic diagnosis or stage change has been established.

Answer and explanation

A. indeterminate

Answer and explanation

C. reviewing

Review is the current action, not a completed metastatic diagnosis.

3. Patient: "Spread has not been ___."

Answer and explanation

B. established

The case provides no conclusion that the finding represents cancer spread.

4. Clinician: "No stage change is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. confirmed

A stage change cannot be inferred from the uncertain finding alone.

differentiation: Degree to which tumor cells resemble normal cells of their origin. / local extent: Spread of a tumor within its original area. / regional lymph node: Lymph node in the region draining or associated with the tumor site. / distant metastasis: Cancer spread to a site distant from the primary tumor. / clinical stage: Stage based on clinical evaluation before definitive pathological staging when applicable. / pathological stage: Stage incorporating surgical and pathological findings where applicable. / TNM classification: Cancer-specific classification describing tumor, regional nodes, and distant metastasis. / stage group: Overall stage category combining applicable staging information. / staging investigation: Test used to help determine cancer extent. / imaging finding: Observation reported from a diagnostic image. / indeterminate finding: Finding whose nature or significance is not yet established. / nodal involvement: Presence of cancer in lymph nodes. / localized disease: Cancer confined to a defined local site in the relevant context. / metastatic disease: Cancer that has spread to distant body sites. / prognostic factor: Feature associated with a disease's expected course or outcome. / staging completion: Point at which the necessary staging information has been assembled. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Lesson 02: Separating stage, grade, and prognosis
Original fictional case: Theo has a pathology report that includes a tumor grade. Imaging needed for staging is still pending. Dr Malik must explain that grade concerns microscopic features while stage describes extent using the applicable cancer-specific system. No grade value, stage, or individual survival estimate is supplied. Theo has mistaken the grade for a completed stage.
Language workshop: Two terms on one report
Communication goal: Explain closely related oncology terms without converting one into another.
Language guidance: Grade and stage are not synonyms. Staging systems differ by cancer type; do not assign a TNM category or stage from a vocabulary definition.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

03Discussing biomarker resultsA possible match is not a promise+

Your goal

Explain the difference between a molecular finding, a relevant option, and treatment eligibility.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

A possible match is not a promise

A tumor biomarker report identifies a potentially relevant molecular alteration for Quinn. Dr Sato has not established that a particular treatment is appropriate or available. Clinical context, the strength of evidence, and access need review. The report is tumor testing; no inherited finding has been established. Quinn hopes the result guarantees an effective targeted treatment.

Dr Sato: Oncologist / Quinn: Patient

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the report identify?

Answer and explanation

D. A potentially relevant tumor alteration

The molecular finding may be relevant, but further interpretation is still required.

2. What is not established?

Answer and explanation

A. Suitability or availability of a particular treatment

The case leaves appropriateness and access unresolved rather than promising a treatment.

3. What kind of testing is described?

Answer and explanation

A. Tumor testing

The report concerns the tumor and does not establish an inherited finding.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A molecular alteration does not guarantee a useful treatment match or response. A tumor finding is not automatically an inherited finding.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
biomarker
Measurable feature providing information about a biological process or disease.interpret a tumor biomarker
molecular alteration
Change in a molecular feature such as DNA sequence or gene activity.characterize a molecular alteration
targeted therapy
Treatment directed at a relevant molecular target involved in disease.evaluate a targeted therapy
somatic variant
Genetic change acquired in body cells rather than inherited through germ cells.explain a somatic variant
germline variant
Genetic change in the inherited genetic material that may be passed to offspring.assess a germline variant
variant of uncertain significance
Genetic change whose clinical meaning is not established.report a variant of uncertain significance
actionability
Degree to which a finding can usefully inform a clinical action.assess clinical actionability
evidence level
Strength or category of support for a clinical claim or action.review the evidence level
companion diagnostic
Test providing information essential for appropriate use of a corresponding treatment.verify a companion diagnostic
tumor profiling
Characterization of molecular features of a tumor.review tumor profiling
sequencing panel
Test examining a selected set of genetic targets.interpret a sequencing panel
specimen adequacy
Whether a sample meets the requirements for a reliable analysis.confirm specimen adequacy
tumor heterogeneity
Differences among cells or regions within or between tumors.discuss tumor heterogeneity
acquired resistance
Reduced treatment sensitivity developing during or after exposure.investigate acquired resistance
predictive biomarker
Feature associated with likelihood of response to a particular treatment.interpret a predictive biomarker
genetic counseling
Professional support in understanding genetic findings and their implications.arrange genetic counseling
testing limitation
Restriction on what a test can detect or establish.explain a testing limitation
treatment access
Practical ability to obtain an appropriate treatment.clarify treatment access
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Uncertain significance means the clinical meaning remains unresolved. Do not call a variant actionable solely because it appears in a detailed report.

C Notice the language

Hope does not require a guarantee

Acknowledge why a possible treatment match matters. Explain the remaining checks without presenting them as bureaucratic obstacles or erasing the potential relevance.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge hope
I understand why a possible match feels important.
State the finding
The report identifies a tumor alteration that may be relevant.
Separate two steps
Finding a target and choosing a treatment are different steps.
Limit a promise
This does not guarantee that a treatment will work.
Explain review
We need to assess the evidence and your clinical circumstances.
Separate inheritance
Tumor testing does not automatically establish an inherited change.
Name access
We also need to establish whether an appropriate option is available.
Protect uncertainty
Uncertain significance is not the same as proven actionability.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Potentially relevant
States possible significance without claiming a confirmed treatment indication.
Does not automatically
Prevents a tumor result from being treated as proof of inherited risk.
Need to establish
Marks a necessary unresolved check rather than a promised outcome.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which statement preserves the result accurately?

Answer and explanation

A. The alteration may be relevant; suitability still needs review.

May be relevant preserves the finding without treating unresolved suitability as an established treatment choice.

2. Which distinction matters for relatives?

Answer and explanation

A. Tumor findings are not automatically inherited findings.

The type of testing and further assessment determine whether inherited implications are established.

3. What does uncertain significance mean?

Answer and explanation

C. Clinical meaning is not established.

The phrase preserves uncertainty rather than supporting a specific action by itself.

4. Which additional issue belongs in the discussion?

Answer and explanation

B. Evidence, clinical context, and treatment access

Those unresolved factors affect whether a possible match becomes a useful clinical option.

E Conversations

A possible match is not a promise

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Dr Sato: Oncologist / Quinn: Patient

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • genetic counseling
  • evidence level
  • testing limitation
  • variant of uncertain significance
  • treatment access
  • molecular alteration
  • actionability
  • germline variant
  • somatic variant
  • targeted therapy
  1. Dr Sato
    The tumor report is available. What have you understood from it so far, and what would you like clarified?
  2. Quinn
    It lists a 1. I hoped that meant there was a medicine certain to work.
  3. Dr Sato
    I understand that hope. The finding may be relevant, but it is the start of another clinical discussion.
  4. Quinn
    Is this the difference between a finding and its 2, rather than just a complicated way to say the same thing?
  5. Dr Sato
    Yes. We need to review the supporting evidence and whether the finding fits an appropriate option for you.
  6. Quinn
    I would like you to explain the 3. A long report can look more certain than the information really is.
  7. Dr Sato
    That is a fair request. A possible target does not guarantee a response to the associated treatment.
  8. Quinn
    Then 4 describes how an option works, not a promise of benefit for me.
  9. Dr Sato
    Correct. The clinical setting and other factors matter, and we also need to establish availability.
  10. Quinn
    Please include 5 in that discussion. I cannot assume something listed in a report is obtainable here.
  11. Dr Sato
    We will. You also asked whether this result means your children have the same genetic change.
  12. Quinn
    Yes. I was unsure whether a 6 was the same as something inherited through the family.
  13. Dr Sato
    It is not the same category. This is tumor testing; it does not automatically establish an inherited finding.
  14. Quinn
    So a 7 would require the appropriate separate interpretation and, where indicated, further assessment.
  15. Dr Sato
    Exactly. We can discuss a genetics referral if appropriate, without declaring an inherited result that we do not have.
  16. Quinn
    I also noticed a different entry marked 8. I should not treat that as another proven treatment target.
  17. Dr Sato
    Correct. We will explain the limits of the test as well as the findings it reports.
  18. Quinn
    A 9 is important to me because I do not want a negative entry interpreted as an answer to every question.
  19. Dr Sato
    We will review those limits, the possible options, and the next steps together before making recommendations.
  20. Quinn
    And 10 can help if the inherited-risk question needs specialist discussion, rather than me guessing from the report.

Answers and explanations
  1. molecular alteration Turn 2. A molecular alteration is a change in a molecular feature and does not alone establish an effective treatment.
  2. actionability Turn 4. Actionability concerns whether a finding can usefully inform a clinical action in the actual context.
  3. evidence level Turn 6. Evidence level describes the strength of support for a proposed clinical use, which can vary between findings.
  4. targeted therapy Turn 8. Targeted therapy acts on a relevant molecular target but is not a guarantee of effectiveness for every patient.
  5. treatment access Turn 10. Treatment access concerns whether an appropriate option can actually be obtained, separate from a report's potential match.
  6. somatic variant Turn 12. A somatic variant is acquired in body cells and is distinct from a change established as inherited.
  7. germline variant Turn 14. A germline variant concerns inherited genetic material, which has not been established by this tumor report alone.
  8. variant of uncertain significance Turn 16. A variant of uncertain significance has an unresolved clinical meaning and should not be presented as a proven treatment target.
  9. testing limitation Turn 18. A testing limitation restricts what the analysis can detect or establish and belongs in a balanced interpretation.
  10. genetic counseling Turn 20. Genetic counseling provides qualified support for understanding genetic findings and implications without assuming inherited risk has already been proved.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Dr Sato: The tumor report is available. What have you understood from it so far, and what would you like clarified?
  2. Quinn: It lists a molecular alteration. I hoped that meant there was a medicine certain to work.
  3. Dr Sato: I understand that hope. The finding may be relevant, but it is the start of another clinical discussion.
  4. Quinn: Is this the difference between a finding and its actionability, rather than just a complicated way to say the same thing?
  5. Dr Sato: Yes. We need to review the supporting evidence and whether the finding fits an appropriate option for you.
  6. Quinn: I would like you to explain the evidence level. A long report can look more certain than the information really is.
  7. Dr Sato: That is a fair request. A possible target does not guarantee a response to the associated treatment.
  8. Quinn: Then targeted therapy describes how an option works, not a promise of benefit for me.
  9. Dr Sato: Correct. The clinical setting and other factors matter, and we also need to establish availability.
  10. Quinn: Please include treatment access in that discussion. I cannot assume something listed in a report is obtainable here.
  11. Dr Sato: We will. You also asked whether this result means your children have the same genetic change.
  12. Quinn: Yes. I was unsure whether a somatic variant was the same as something inherited through the family.
  13. Dr Sato: It is not the same category. This is tumor testing; it does not automatically establish an inherited finding.
  14. Quinn: So a germline variant would require the appropriate separate interpretation and, where indicated, further assessment.
  15. Dr Sato: Exactly. We can discuss a genetics referral if appropriate, without declaring an inherited result that we do not have.
  16. Quinn: I also noticed a different entry marked variant of uncertain significance. I should not treat that as another proven treatment target.
  17. Dr Sato: Correct. We will explain the limits of the test as well as the findings it reports.
  18. Quinn: A testing limitation is important to me because I do not want a negative entry interpreted as an answer to every question.
  19. Dr Sato: We will review those limits, the possible options, and the next steps together before making recommendations.
  20. Quinn: And genetic counseling can help if the inherited-risk question needs specialist discussion, rather than me guessing from the report.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A biomarker is not a treatment guarantee

A patient asks about an identified alteration.

  1. Patient: The report names an alteration. Does that guarantee a targeted treatment will work for me?
  2. Oncologist: No. A biomarker may inform options, but it does not guarantee response or availability.
  3. Patient: I also want to know whether the finding is established and clinically relevant.
  4. Oncologist: We should review the actual test, interpretation, and evidence for this disease context.
  5. Patient: Could the same alteration have different implications in different kinds of cancer?
  6. Oncologist: The clinical context matters; a familiar term is not a universal treatment instruction.
  7. Patient: Then we need to distinguish a possible option from an agreed treatment plan.
  8. Oncologist: Exactly, including eligibility, access, benefits, harms, and the actual clinical assessment.
  9. Patient: Please do not let an encouraging headline replace that individualized explanation.
  10. Oncologist: We will explain what the result supports and what remains uncertain before discussing a decision.
Tumor testing and inherited risk

A patient asks whether a tumor result proves a family risk.

  1. Patient: The tumor test found a change, and I assumed that means my children inherited it.
  2. Oncologist: A tumor finding does not automatically establish an inherited variant in your family.
  3. Patient: Could you distinguish a somatic change from a germline finding?
  4. Oncologist: A somatic change is acquired in cells; a germline finding concerns inherited genetic material.
  5. Patient: Does that mean the tumor report alone cannot settle whether relatives need testing?
  6. Oncologist: Correct. The appropriate assessment and counseling determine what further discussion is relevant.
  7. Patient: I would like to know what information is confirmed rather than alarm everyone from an assumption.
  8. Oncologist: We should explain the actual result and the limits of what it establishes.
  9. Patient: Who can help us understand any genuine inherited-risk question that remains?
  10. Oncologist: The appropriate genetics service can provide counseling through the actual referral process.
A variant remains uncertain

Two clinicians clarify how an uncertain variant should be described.

  1. Oncologist: The report lists a variant of uncertain significance, and the patient thinks it proves a harmful mutation.
  2. Genetics clinician: We should explain that its clinical significance is not established by that label.
  3. Oncologist: I will avoid presenting the variant as a confirmed explanation for the disease.
  4. Genetics clinician: That is important. The uncertainty should remain visible in the patient discussion and record.
  5. Oncologist: Does the wording itself authorize a treatment change or family testing plan?
  6. Genetics clinician: No. The actual clinical and genetics assessment must determine what action, if any, is appropriate.
  7. Oncologist: The patient would benefit from a clear explanation of why uncertain does not mean harmless either.
  8. Genetics clinician: Agreed. Neither pathogenic nor benign should be asserted without the supporting interpretation.
  9. Oncologist: I will identify the appropriate contact for questions about the report and any later update.
  10. Genetics clinician: That keeps the result understandable without forcing a conclusion the evidence does not support.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A possible match is not a promise
  1. Read the corrected biomarker discussion. Contrast a finding, a possible option, and a guaranteed response.
  2. Swap roles. Repeat the somatic and germline distinction without declaring an inherited result.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A result with insufficient sample

A profiling report says the sample was insufficient for one analysis. The result for that target is unavailable, not negative. A clinician will discuss whether further testing is appropriate.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Patient: "The sample was ___ for that analysis."

Answer and explanation

C. insufficient

The report describes a sample limitation, not an absent molecular target.

2. Clinician: "The target result is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. unavailable

The analysis did not produce an interpretable result for that target.

3. Patient: "That is not the same as a ___ result."

Answer and explanation

B. negative

Failure to obtain a result cannot be converted into a negative finding.

4. Clinician: "Further testing needs clinical ___."

Answer and explanation

C. review

The next testing decision requires assessment rather than automatic repetition.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.
