Lesson 03: Discussing biomarker results
Original fictional case: A tumor biomarker report identifies a potentially relevant molecular alteration for Quinn. Dr Sato has not established that a particular treatment is appropriate or available. Clinical context, the strength of evidence, and access need review. The report is tumor testing; no inherited finding has been established. Quinn hopes the result guarantees an effective targeted treatment.
Language workshop: A possible match is not a promise
Communication goal: Explain the difference between a molecular finding, a relevant option, and treatment eligibility.
Language guidance: A molecular alteration does not guarantee a useful treatment match or response. A tumor finding is not automatically an inherited finding.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I understand why a possible match feels important. / The report identifies a tumor alteration that may be relevant. / Finding a target and choosing a treatment are different steps. / This does not guarantee that a treatment will work. / We need to assess the evidence and your clinical circumstances. / Tumor testing does not automatically establish an inherited change. / We also need to establish whether an appropriate option is available. / Uncertain significance is not the same as proven actionability. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: biomarker: Measurable feature providing information about a biological process or disease. / molecular alteration: Change in a molecular feature such as DNA sequence or gene activity. / targeted therapy: Treatment directed at a relevant molecular target involved in disease. / somatic variant: Genetic change acquired in body cells rather than inherited through germ cells. / germline variant: Genetic change in the inherited genetic material that may be passed to offspring. / variant of uncertain significance: Genetic change whose clinical meaning is not established. / actionability: Degree to which a finding can usefully inform a clinical action. / evidence level: Strength or category of support for a clinical claim or action. / companion diagnostic: Test providing information essential for appropriate use of a corresponding treatment. / tumor profiling: Characterization of molecular features of a tumor. / sequencing panel: Test examining a selected set of genetic targets. / specimen adequacy: Whether a sample meets the requirements for a reliable analysis. / tumor heterogeneity: Differences among cells or regions within or between tumors. / acquired resistance: Reduced treatment sensitivity developing during or after exposure. / predictive biomarker: Feature associated with likelihood of response to a particular treatment. / genetic counseling: Professional support in understanding genetic findings and their implications. / testing limitation: Restriction on what a test can detect or establish. / treatment access: Practical ability to obtain an appropriate treatment. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected biomarker discussion. Contrast a finding, a possible option, and a guaranteed response. Swap roles. Repeat the somatic and germline distinction without declaring an inherited result. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Dr Sato: Oncologist / Quinn: Patient
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A profiling report says the sample was insufficient for one analysis. The result for that target is unavailable, not negative. A clinician will discuss whether further testing is appropriate.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Dr Sato: Correct. We will explain the limits of the test as well as the findings it reports.
Quinn: A testing limitation is important to me because I do not want a negative entry interpreted as an answer to every question.
Dr Sato: We will review those limits, the possible options, and the next steps together before making recommendations.
Quinn: And genetic counseling can help if the inherited-risk question needs specialist discussion, rather than me guessing from the report.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.

3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Making treatment intent explicitWhat is this option trying to achieve?+

Your goal

Explain treatment goals and tradeoffs without promising a response or presenting consent as a formality.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

What is this option trying to achieve?

Dr Bell discusses a proposed treatment option with Jordan. In this fictional case its stated aim is disease control, not a promised cure. The benefits, burdens, alternatives, and practical effects still need an individual discussion. Jordan prioritizes being able to care for a partner. No regimen, dose, response probability, or final decision is supplied.

Jordan: Patient / Dr Bell: Oncologist

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the proposed aim?

Answer and explanation

C. Disease control

The case names disease control and explicitly excludes a promised cure.

2. What matters particularly to Jordan?

Answer and explanation

B. Caring for a partner

Jordan's caregiving role is the stated personal priority to explore.

3. What remains undecided?

Answer and explanation

A. The final treatment choice

The discussion is ongoing and no final treatment decision is supplied.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Curative intent states an aim, not a guarantee. Disease control and symptom relief are also meaningful but different goals.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
treatment intent
Goal a treatment is intended to pursue.clarify treatment intent
curative intent
Treatment aim of eliminating the cancer.explain curative intent
disease control
Limiting cancer growth or spread as a treatment goal.discuss disease control
neoadjuvant treatment
Treatment given before the main local treatment in an applicable plan.explain neoadjuvant treatment
adjuvant treatment
Additional treatment after a primary treatment to reduce recurrence risk when appropriate.discuss adjuvant treatment
systemic therapy
Treatment acting throughout the body rather than only at one local site.explain systemic therapy
local treatment
Treatment directed at a particular body area.discuss local treatment
treatment regimen
Planned combination and schedule of treatment components.review the treatment regimen
treatment cycle
Repeated unit of a planned treatment schedule.explain a treatment cycle
performance status
Clinical description of a person's activity and functional ability.assess performance status
quality of life
Person's experienced well-being across relevant areas of life.discuss quality of life
treatment burden
Physical, practical, and emotional demands associated with treatment.assess treatment burden
benefit-risk balance
Weighing expected benefits against possible harms.explain the benefit-risk balance
alternative option
Another reasonable approach available for discussion.compare alternative options
supportive treatment
Intervention addressing symptoms or treatment-related difficulties.coordinate supportive treatment
informed consent
Voluntary agreement after an appropriate explanation and opportunity for questions.support informed consent
decision capacity
Ability to make the relevant decision under the applicable assessment.assess decision capacity
caregiving responsibility
Duties involved in helping another person with care.discuss caregiving responsibilities
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

A signature does not replace a discussion of the proposed intervention, alternatives, benefits, risks, and the person's questions. This exercise supplies no treatment selection rule.

C Notice the language

Ask what the goal means in daily life

A treatment goal has both a clinical meaning and a personal significance. A patient may value time at home, symptom relief, or a particular activity differently from the clinician's initial assumption.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the aim
The aim of this option is to control the disease.
Avoid a guarantee
An intended benefit is not a promised outcome.
Ask about daily life
How would the treatment demands affect caring for your partner?
Explain the burden
We should discuss visits, symptoms, and practical demands together.
Invite alternatives
Let us compare the reasonable alternatives for your circumstances.
Keep choice open
You have not made a final decision in this discussion.
Check the goal
What do you understand this option is trying to achieve?
Respect questions
Questions are part of the consent discussion, not an obstacle to it.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
The aim is
States intended purpose without claiming an achieved result.
Would affect
Explores a possible practical consequence before a final decision.
Not a guarantee
Prevents treatment-intent language from becoming a promised outcome.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which statement accurately names the goal?

Answer and explanation

D. This option aims at disease control.

Disease control is the stated aim; no response or cure is promised.

2. Which question elicits a relevant priority?

Answer and explanation

A. How would the treatment demands affect caring for your partner?

Asking about the effect on caregiving links treatment demands with Jordan's stated priority.

3. Which account of consent is incomplete?

Answer and explanation

D. A signature alone proves understanding.

Signing a form cannot replace adequate explanation and attention to understanding and choice.

4. Which distinction is correct?

Answer and explanation

A. Curative intent is an aim, not a guarantee.

Intent describes the goal pursued and does not establish that it will be achieved.

Check explanations in the book, page 91 ↗

E Conversations

What is this option trying to achieve?

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Jordan: Patient / Dr Bell: Oncologist

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • treatment regimen
  • treatment intent
  • quality of life
  • alternative option
  • Disease control
  • caregiving responsibility
  • Informed consent
  • benefit-risk balance
  • Treatment burden
  • treatment cycle
  1. Jordan
    Before we go through appointments, I need to understand what you hope this treatment will do.
  2. Dr Bell
    The 1 here is disease control. I am not describing it as a guaranteed cure.
  3. Jordan
    That is clearer than saying it will help. Does control mean stopping everything permanently?
  4. Dr Bell
    2 means trying to limit growth or spread; the possible benefit and uncertainty need an individual discussion.
  5. Jordan
    I care for my partner. Repeated hospital visits could affect both of us, not only me.
  6. Dr Bell
    Your 3 belongs in this conversation. Let us examine those demands alongside the clinical aims.
  7. Jordan
    I do not want practical difficulties to sound as though I am refusing treatment without listening.
  8. Dr Bell
    They do not. 4 includes visits and disruption as well as physical effects.
  9. Jordan
    Could you explain the proposed schedule, then distinguish what is definite from what may change?
  10. Dr Bell
    We would review the 5 and the circumstances in which it might need review.
  11. Jordan
    I have heard cycle used as though everyone knows what it means. Please explain that too.
  12. Dr Bell
    A 6 is a repeated unit of the schedule. Its details depend on the actual plan.
  13. Jordan
    I would also like to understand the other reasonable options, not just the one named first.
  14. Dr Bell
    We should compare each 7, including the expected benefits, limitations, and demands relevant to you.
  15. Jordan
    Being able to function at home matters to me. A scan is not the only thing I will judge by.
  16. Dr Bell
    Your 8 is important. We need to understand the outcomes that matter to you.
  17. Jordan
    I am not ready to sign anything just because an appointment slot might be available.
  18. Dr Bell
    9 is a discussion and a voluntary decision, not simply obtaining a signature.
  19. Jordan
    Then let us compare the possible benefit with the burdens before calling this a settled plan.
  20. Dr Bell
    Yes. We will review the 10 and your priorities; no final choice has been made in this conversation.

Answers and explanations
  1. treatment intent Turn 2. Treatment intent identifies the goal pursued by an option rather than an outcome that has already occurred.
  2. Disease control Turn 4. Disease control means limiting growth or spread and does not mean a guaranteed permanent elimination of cancer.
  3. caregiving responsibility Turn 6. Caregiving responsibility is a real practical priority that should inform the discussion of treatment demands.
  4. Treatment burden Turn 8. Treatment burden includes physical, practical, and emotional demands and is relevant to an informed treatment choice.
  5. treatment regimen Turn 10. A treatment regimen is the planned combination and schedule, which must be explained for the actual option rather than invented here.
  6. treatment cycle Turn 12. A treatment cycle is a repeating unit of a planned schedule, not a guarantee of response or a universal length.
  7. alternative option Turn 14. An alternative option is another reasonable approach that belongs in the individualized discussion before a voluntary decision.
  8. quality of life Turn 16. Quality of life concerns experienced well-being and functioning, not merely a radiological measure.
  9. Informed consent Turn 18. Informed consent requires a voluntary decision after appropriate explanation and questions, not merely a convenient appointment or signature.
  10. benefit-risk balance Turn 20. The benefit-risk balance weighs potential benefit against possible harm without treating either as certain.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Jordan: Before we go through appointments, I need to understand what you hope this treatment will do.
  2. Dr Bell: The treatment intent here is disease control. I am not describing it as a guaranteed cure.
  3. Jordan: That is clearer than saying it will help. Does control mean stopping everything permanently?
  4. Dr Bell: Disease control means trying to limit growth or spread; the possible benefit and uncertainty need an individual discussion.
  5. Jordan: I care for my partner. Repeated hospital visits could affect both of us, not only me.
  6. Dr Bell: Your caregiving responsibility belongs in this conversation. Let us examine those demands alongside the clinical aims.
  7. Jordan: I do not want practical difficulties to sound as though I am refusing treatment without listening.
  8. Dr Bell: They do not. Treatment burden includes visits and disruption as well as physical effects.
  9. Jordan: Could you explain the proposed schedule, then distinguish what is definite from what may change?
  10. Dr Bell: We would review the treatment regimen and the circumstances in which it might need review.
  11. Jordan: I have heard cycle used as though everyone knows what it means. Please explain that too.
  12. Dr Bell: A treatment cycle is a repeated unit of the schedule. Its details depend on the actual plan.
  13. Jordan: I would also like to understand the other reasonable options, not just the one named first.
  14. Dr Bell: We should compare each alternative option, including the expected benefits, limitations, and demands relevant to you.
  15. Jordan: Being able to function at home matters to me. A scan is not the only thing I will judge by.
  16. Dr Bell: Your quality of life is important. We need to understand the outcomes that matter to you.
  17. Jordan: I am not ready to sign anything just because an appointment slot might be available.
  18. Dr Bell: Informed consent is a discussion and a voluntary decision, not simply obtaining a signature.
  19. Jordan: Then let us compare the possible benefit with the burdens before calling this a settled plan.
  20. Dr Bell: Yes. We will review the benefit-risk balance and your priorities; no final choice has been made in this conversation.
Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Control is not cure

A patient checks the stated treatment intent.

  1. Patient: You described the aim as disease control. I heard that as a promise of cure.
  2. Oncologist: Those are different aims, and I need to correct that misunderstanding clearly.
  3. Patient: Please explain the actual intent without using a more comforting word that changes its meaning.
  4. Oncologist: The relevant plan aims to control the disease; we should discuss what that means in your situation.
  5. Patient: Does an aim describe what is hoped for rather than guarantee what will happen?
  6. Oncologist: Yes. Treatment intent and the eventual outcome must remain distinct.
  7. Patient: I would like the expected benefits and burdens explained against that stated aim.
  8. Oncologist: That belongs in the informed discussion, using the actual options and evidence.
  9. Patient: Then I can compare choices without believing a cure has been promised.
  10. Oncologist: Exactly. Clear language about intent helps you understand the decision rather than merely agree to terminology.
Caregiving responsibilities affect the discussion

A patient explains a practical priority.

  1. Oncologist: What responsibilities or priorities should we include when discussing the actual treatment options?
  2. Patient: I care for my partner, and frequent appointments could affect both of us.
  3. Oncologist: That practical burden belongs in the discussion rather than being dismissed as unrelated.
  4. Patient: I do not want it recorded as though I have already refused treatment.
  5. Oncologist: A concern about feasibility is not the same as an informed decision to decline.
  6. Patient: Could we clarify the actual visit requirements before deciding what support is needed?
  7. Oncologist: Yes. We should verify the proposed schedule and available services rather than guess.
  8. Patient: I would also like someone to help explore support without promising assistance is guaranteed.
  9. Oncologist: We can identify the appropriate service and distinguish an application from confirmed help.
  10. Patient: That would let me discuss the clinical options with the practical facts in view.
Consent after a clearer explanation

A patient requests clarification before agreeing to a plan.

  1. Patient: I signed that I received information, but I still do not understand the treatment goal.
  2. Oncologist: Let us clarify it. A signature should not replace a meaningful discussion.
  3. Patient: I need to distinguish reducing symptoms from reducing the chance of recurrence.
  4. Oncologist: Those are different purposes, and the actual recommendation should identify its intended aim.
  5. Patient: Could you explain which aim applies here rather than list every possible benefit?
  6. Oncologist: Yes, using the real plan and its relevant evidence and limitations.
  7. Patient: I would then like to explain it back so you can check your wording was clear.
  8. Oncologist: That helps us check understanding without treating you as the problem.
  9. Patient: Understanding the explanation does not necessarily mean I have agreed to start immediately.
  10. Oncologist: Correct. Comprehension, questions, and the actual decision should be distinguished and documented accurately.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

What is this option trying to achieve?
  1. Read the corrected options conversation. Contrast intended benefit with promised outcome.
  2. Swap roles. Repeat the caregiving-priority question and the consent explanation without choosing a treatment for Jordan.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A goal before a schedule

Reese asks about a proposed option whose stated aim is symptom relief. The schedule has not been agreed. Reese wants its practical demands explained before deciding.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Clinician: "The stated aim is symptom ___."

Answer and explanation

C. relief

Symptom relief is the goal supplied for this fictional option.

2. Reese: "The schedule is not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

B. agreed

The case explicitly leaves the treatment schedule unsettled.

3. Clinician: "We need to explain the practical ___."

Answer and explanation

B. demands

Reese requests information about the option's practical effect before deciding.

4. Reese: "My decision remains ___."

Answer and explanation

D. open

No final choice is supplied in this ongoing discussion.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

05Escalating a treatment-related symptom reportThe call cannot become a routine message+

Your goal

Give a concise urgent handoff that distinguishes a reported symptom from an established treatment complication.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

The call cannot become a routine message

Oncology nurse Ana calls Dr Ford about Lee, who reports fever and shaking after recent systemic treatment. The urgent oncology assessment pathway has already been activated according to the service protocol. The cause and laboratory results are unknown. The clinicians must exchange the relevant history and confirm responsibility; this case provides no triage threshold, diagnosis, or dosing instruction.

Ana: Oncology nurse / Dr Ford: Oncologist

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is reported?

Answer and explanation

C. Fever and shaking after recent treatment

Lee reports symptoms; the cause has not been established.

2. What action is already taken?

Answer and explanation

B. The urgent assessment pathway is activated

The brief says the service's urgent pathway has already been activated.

3. What remains unknown?

Answer and explanation

C. Cause and laboratory results

The handoff must preserve these unknowns rather than manufacture findings.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Fever after treatment raises a concern requiring the actual clinical pathway; it does not establish neutropenia, infection, or treatment causality by itself.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
treatment-related toxicity
Harmful effect attributed to treatment after appropriate assessment.assess suspected treatment-related toxicity
febrile episode
Period during which fever occurs.report a febrile episode
neutropenia
Abnormally low neutrophil count.assess for neutropenia
neutrophil count
Measurement of the number of neutrophils in blood.review the neutrophil count
immunosuppression
Reduced activity or effectiveness of immune defenses.consider immunosuppression
infection concern
Suspicion that infection may be relevant to a clinical presentation.escalate an infection concern
infusion reaction
Adverse response occurring in association with an infusion.report a suspected infusion reaction
extravasation
Escape of an infused substance from a vessel into surrounding tissue.escalate suspected extravasation
mucositis
Inflammation affecting mucous membranes.describe mucositis symptoms
dehydration
Deficit of body water requiring clinical assessment in context.assess suspected dehydration
adverse event
Unfavorable occurrence during care, not necessarily caused by treatment.report an adverse event
causality assessment
Evaluation of whether one event caused another.preserve uncertainty in causality assessment
symptom onset time
Time when the reported symptom began.confirm symptom onset time
last treatment date
Most recent date on which treatment was administered.verify the last treatment date
urgent assessment
Prompt clinical evaluation through the appropriate service pathway.arrange urgent assessment
receiving clinician
Professional taking responsibility for the next clinical assessment.identify the receiving clinician
read-back
Repetition of important information to verify its accuracy.use read-back for key details
escalation acknowledgment
Confirmation that an urgent request has been received and accepted.obtain escalation acknowledgment
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Do not delay an urgent response to finish a language exercise or obtain a perfect history. This scenario begins after the real service pathway has been activated.

C Notice the language

Urgency and uncertainty can coexist

A clinician can request urgent assessment without claiming a confirmed cause. Lead with the concern and action already taken, then identify the information still needed.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Lead with urgency
I am calling about an urgent symptom report after recent treatment.
State the action
The urgent assessment pathway is already activated.
Attribute the report
Lee reports fever and shaking.
Preserve an unknown
We do not yet have the laboratory results.
Avoid premature diagnosis
I am reporting the concern, not a confirmed cause.
Confirm responsibility
Who is accepting the next assessment?
Read back
I will repeat the key details to check accuracy.
Separate processes
This must not be left as a routine administrative message.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Reports versus has confirmed
Separates the patient's symptom account from an established clinical diagnosis.
Already activated
Marks an action completed before this handoff begins.
Accepting
Makes the transfer of responsibility explicit rather than implying that a message sent itself completes a handoff.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which opening best supports the handoff?

Answer and explanation

D. Urgent symptom report after recent treatment; the pathway is activated.

Naming the urgent report and activated pathway communicates the concern and action without inventing a diagnosis.

2. Which diagnosis cannot be asserted from this brief?

Answer and explanation

A. Confirmed neutropenia

A low neutrophil count has not been established because laboratory results are unknown.

3. What must the handoff confirm?

Answer and explanation

D. The receiving clinician and responsibility

A handoff requires an accepted next step rather than relying on a message's existence.

4. Which statement preserves causality correctly?

Answer and explanation

D. Symptoms occurred after treatment; the cause is not established.

Temporal sequence does not prove causality and does not remove the need for urgent assessment.

Check explanations in the book, page 93 ↗

E Conversations

The call cannot become a routine message

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ana: Oncology nurse / Dr Ford: Oncologist

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • causality assessment
  • urgent assessment
  • escalation acknowledgment
  • adverse event
  • read-back
  • receiving clinician
  • infection concern
  • symptom onset time
  • last treatment date
  • neutrophil count
  1. Ana
    I am calling about Lee, who reports fever and shaking after recent systemic treatment. The urgent pathway is activated.
  2. Dr Ford
    Thank you. Keep this with 1, not a routine message. Tell me the verified details and what remains unknown.
  3. Ana
    The symptoms are patient-reported. I am confirming their timing, and we do not yet have laboratory results.
  4. Dr Ford
    Please preserve the 2 as reported, including any uncertainty in the account.
  5. Ana
    I will also verify the most recent treatment information from the actual record rather than rely on memory.
  6. Dr Ford
    Yes. The 3 matters, but do not delay the active response while completing every detail.
  7. Ana
    Shall I describe this as confirmed treatment toxicity in the handoff heading, or keep the cause open?
  8. Dr Ford
    Keep the 4 open. The symptoms followed treatment, but we have not established their cause.
  9. Ana
    I have not labeled Lee neutropenic. There is no count available to support that description yet.
  10. Dr Ford
    Correct. A 5 is not available; do not fill that gap with an assumed result.
  11. Ana
    The clinical concern remains urgent even while we avoid assigning a diagnosis that has not been established.
  12. Dr Ford
    Exactly. We can escalate an 6 without declaring that infection is confirmed.
  13. Ana
    I need a clear receiving contact so that the report does not move between teams without an owner.
  14. Dr Ford
    The 7 must be identified through our pathway. I am accepting this clinical discussion now.
  15. Ana
    I will repeat the patient's reported symptoms and the actions already taken, then confirm the next communication.
  16. Dr Ford
    Use 8 for the important details and correct anything that differs from the record.
  17. Ana
    I will document that the concern was received and who accepted it, not simply that a call was attempted.
  18. Dr Ford
    That 9 makes the handoff clear. Keep any new information with the active clinical team.
  19. Ana
    Understood. The urgent pathway remains active, the cause is unresolved, and we are not inventing missing results.
  20. Dr Ford
    Correct. Record the 10 factually and follow the actual clinical and reporting procedures.

Answers and explanations
  1. urgent assessment Turn 2. Urgent assessment is the activated clinical pathway in this case, not a routine message to be reviewed later.
  2. symptom onset time Turn 4. Symptom onset time identifies when the symptoms began and must be confirmed rather than guessed during the handoff.
  3. last treatment date Turn 6. The last treatment date provides relevant clinical context and should be verified from the appropriate source.
  4. causality assessment Turn 8. Causality assessment determines whether treatment caused an event; that conclusion is not supplied by timing alone.
  5. neutrophil count Turn 10. A neutrophil count is a laboratory measurement that has not yet been supplied in this scenario.
  6. infection concern Turn 12. An infection concern is a clinical suspicion requiring assessment, not a confirmed infection diagnosis.
  7. receiving clinician Turn 14. The receiving clinician is the professional accepting the next clinical assessment and responsibility in the handoff.
  8. read-back Turn 16. Read-back repeats important details to confirm accuracy rather than assuming the message was understood correctly.
  9. escalation acknowledgment Turn 18. Escalation acknowledgment records that the urgent request was received and accepted, beyond merely attempting contact.
  10. adverse event Turn 20. An adverse event is an unfavorable occurrence during care and does not by itself prove treatment causation.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ana: I am calling about Lee, who reports fever and shaking after recent systemic treatment. The urgent pathway is activated.
  2. Dr Ford: Thank you. Keep this with urgent assessment, not a routine message. Tell me the verified details and what remains unknown.
  3. Ana: The symptoms are patient-reported. I am confirming their timing, and we do not yet have laboratory results.
  4. Dr Ford: Please preserve the symptom onset time as reported, including any uncertainty in the account.
  5. Ana: I will also verify the most recent treatment information from the actual record rather than rely on memory.
  6. Dr Ford: Yes. The last treatment date matters, but do not delay the active response while completing every detail.
  7. Ana: Shall I describe this as confirmed treatment toxicity in the handoff heading, or keep the cause open?
  8. Dr Ford: Keep the causality assessment open. The symptoms followed treatment, but we have not established their cause.
  9. Ana: I have not labeled Lee neutropenic. There is no count available to support that description yet.
  10. Dr Ford: Correct. A neutrophil count is not available; do not fill that gap with an assumed result.
  11. Ana: The clinical concern remains urgent even while we avoid assigning a diagnosis that has not been established.
  12. Dr Ford: Exactly. We can escalate an infection concern without declaring that infection is confirmed.
  13. Ana: I need a clear receiving contact so that the report does not move between teams without an owner.
  14. Dr Ford: The receiving clinician must be identified through our pathway. I am accepting this clinical discussion now.
  15. Ana: I will repeat the patient's reported symptoms and the actions already taken, then confirm the next communication.
  16. Dr Ford: Use read-back for the important details and correct anything that differs from the record.
  17. Ana: I will document that the concern was received and who accepted it, not simply that a call was attempted.
  18. Dr Ford: That escalation acknowledgment makes the handoff clear. Keep any new information with the active clinical team.
  19. Ana: Understood. The urgent pathway remains active, the cause is unresolved, and we are not inventing missing results.
  20. Dr Ford: Correct. Record the adverse event factually and follow the actual clinical and reporting procedures.
Dialogue in the book, page 46 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A symptom report during an active response

A nurse transfers an urgent treatment-related concern.

  1. Nurse: The patient reports fever and shaking after treatment, and the urgent clinical pathway is already active.
  2. Oncologist: Please lead with those symptoms and the action taken, then identify the verified information.
  3. Nurse: I have not assigned a cause or inserted laboratory values that are not available.
  4. Oncologist: Good. A concerning report should not become a confirmed diagnosis without assessment.
  5. Nurse: The patient also corrected the time symptoms began, and I have recorded that change.
  6. Oncologist: Please preserve the corrected timing and its source in the handoff.
  7. Nurse: Should I wait until every field is complete before confirming the receiving team?
  8. Oncologist: No. The actual urgent response must not wait for a perfectly polished report.
  9. Nurse: I will confirm acceptance and pass new verified information through the active route.
  10. Oncologist: That keeps urgency, uncertainty, and responsibility clear while the clinical assessment continues.
New symptoms are not automatically progression

A patient asks what a new symptom means.

  1. Patient: I have a new symptom during treatment. Does that automatically mean the cancer is progressing?
  2. Oncologist: No. The cause requires the actual clinical assessment; the timing alone does not establish progression.
  3. Patient: I also wondered whether every symptom during treatment must be caused by the medicine.
  4. Oncologist: That is another assumption we should avoid. Report the symptom and follow the actual care instructions.
  5. Patient: I can describe when it began and how it changed without choosing the cause myself.
  6. Oncologist: Those details are useful, with anything measured distinguished from what you noticed.
  7. Patient: I do not want uncertainty about the cause to be mistaken for permission to ignore it.
  8. Oncologist: It should not be. The appropriate clinical response depends on the actual situation.
  9. Patient: Please confirm the real contact route rather than give a general instruction to wait.
  10. Oncologist: We will use your actual care plan and assessment, not a guessed rule from terminology.
An urgent message is not a routine inbox item

Two staff members clarify the communication route.

  1. Coordinator: This message describes an urgent concern, but it arrived in the routine appointment inbox.
  2. Nurse: We need to follow the actual escalation process rather than leave it for ordinary scheduling.
  3. Coordinator: I will not decide that it can wait because the patient used the wrong channel.
  4. Nurse: Correct. The clinical concern needs the appropriate assessment and response.
  5. Coordinator: The message has been forwarded, but I have not received acknowledgment from the clinical team.
  6. Nurse: Then we must distinguish sent from accepted and continue the actual service process.
  7. Coordinator: I will record the source, time, and actions without adding an unconfirmed diagnosis.
  8. Nurse: Please also identify who is responsible for confirming the receiving team.
  9. Coordinator: A forwarded message alone is not a completed clinical handoff.
  10. Nurse: Exactly. The communication loop must support the real response rather than merely move the message elsewhere.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The call cannot become a routine message
  1. Read the corrected urgent handoff. Lead with the concern and the pathway already activated.
  2. Swap roles. Repeat the unknown-results statement and the acknowledgment without inventing a diagnosis or a dose.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Confirm a received escalation

The urgent pathway is active for a new symptom report. Dr Lane accepts the handoff. Laboratory results remain pending; the cause is not established.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Nurse: "The urgent pathway is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. active

The scenario begins with the actual urgent response already in progress.

2. Dr Lane: "I ___ the handoff."

Answer and explanation

D. accept

Dr Lane explicitly accepts responsibility for receiving the clinical handoff.

3. Nurse: "The laboratory results remain ___."

Answer and explanation

D. pending

The results have not yet become available in the supplied facts.

4. Dr Lane: "The cause is not ___."

Answer and explanation

A. established

Neither the symptom report nor the handoff establishes a cause by itself.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I am calling about an urgent symptom report after recent treatment. / The urgent assessment pathway is already activated. / Lee reports fever and shaking. / We do not yet have the laboratory results. / I am reporting the concern, not a confirmed cause. / Who is accepting the next assessment? / I will repeat the key details to check accuracy. / This must not be left as a routine administrative message. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: treatment-related toxicity: Harmful effect attributed to treatment after appropriate assessment. / febrile episode: Period during which fever occurs. / neutropenia: Abnormally low neutrophil count. / neutrophil count: Measurement of the number of neutrophils in blood. / immunosuppression: Reduced activity or effectiveness of immune defenses. / infection concern: Suspicion that infection may be relevant to a clinical presentation. / infusion reaction: Adverse response occurring in association with an infusion. / extravasation: Escape of an infused substance from a vessel into surrounding tissue. / mucositis: Inflammation affecting mucous membranes. / dehydration: Deficit of body water requiring clinical assessment in context. / adverse event: Unfavorable occurrence during care, not necessarily caused by treatment. / causality assessment: Evaluation of whether one event caused another. / symptom onset time: Time when the reported symptom began. / last treatment date: Most recent date on which treatment was administered. / urgent assessment: Prompt clinical evaluation through the appropriate service pathway. / receiving clinician: Professional taking responsibility for the next clinical assessment. / read-back: Repetition of important information to verify its accuracy. / escalation acknowledgment: Confirmation that an urgent request has been received and accepted. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected urgent handoff. Lead with the concern and the pathway already activated. Swap roles. Repeat the unknown-results statement and the acknowledgment without inventing a diagnosis or a dose. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Ana: Oncology nurse / Dr Ford: Oncologist
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: The urgent pathway is active for a new symptom report. Dr Lane accepts the handoff. Laboratory results remain pending; the cause is not established.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ana: I will document that the concern was received and who accepted it, not simply that a call was attempted.
Dr Ford: That escalation acknowledgment makes the handoff clear. Keep any new information with the active clinical team.
Ana: Understood. The urgent pathway remains active, the cause is unresolved, and we are not inventing missing results.
Dr Ford: Correct. Record the adverse event factually and follow the actual clinical and reporting procedures.
END REFERENCE
06Discussing a response-assessment scanSmaller is useful, but not the whole conclusion+

Your goal

Explain a scan comparison while distinguishing measurement, interpretation, and an agreed treatment decision.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

Smaller is useful, but not the whole conclusion

Pat's report describes a measured lesion as smaller than on the comparison scan. The complete response assessment is still under oncology review, and no formal response category or treatment change has been agreed. Dr Ng must acknowledge the encouraging observation without declaring cure, complete response, or a settled next regimen.

Pat: Patient / Dr Ng: Oncologist

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the report describe?

Answer and explanation

C. A measured lesion is smaller

Only the specific size comparison is supplied, not a complete response conclusion.

2. What is still under review?

Answer and explanation

D. The complete response assessment

The complete assessment has not yet been finalized by oncology.

3. What has not been agreed?

Answer and explanation

C. A treatment change

The brief supplies no agreed change to the treatment plan.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A smaller lesion is an observation, not automatically a formal partial response. Complete response and cure are not interchangeable claims.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
response assessment
Evaluation of how disease has changed during or after treatment.complete a response assessment
comparison scan
Earlier imaging study used as a reference.review the comparison scan
measurable lesion
Abnormality that can be measured under the relevant assessment criteria.identify a measurable lesion
target lesion
Selected lesion followed for measurement under a response-assessment system.measure a target lesion
non-target lesion
Disease finding assessed outside the selected target-lesion measurements.assess non-target lesions
partial response
Reduction meeting the relevant criteria without a complete response.explain a partial response
complete response
Disappearance of detectable disease findings under the applicable response criteria.qualify a complete response
stable disease
Disease not meeting the relevant response or progression thresholds.discuss stable disease
progressive disease
Worsening that meets applicable progression criteria.evaluate progressive disease
new lesion
Newly identified abnormality requiring interpretation in context.investigate a new lesion
radiological response
Change in disease appearance assessed using imaging.describe radiological response
clinical response
Change assessed through clinical symptoms, signs, or functioning.compare clinical response
remission
Reduction or disappearance of signs of disease, described as partial or complete when appropriate.explain remission
recurrence
Return of cancer after a period of improvement or remission.discuss recurrence
measurement variability
Differences arising from how measurements are obtained or interpreted.account for measurement variability
scan interval
Time between imaging examinations.confirm the scan interval
restaging
Reassessment of disease extent after treatment or a change.explain restaging
assessment criteria
Defined rules used to classify findings or changes.apply the relevant assessment criteria
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Use the applicable cancer-specific assessment criteria and full clinical context. The exercise supplies no measurement thresholds and cannot classify a real scan.

C Notice the language

Share the useful finding without expanding its meaning

Patients deserve to hear favorable observations clearly. Precision does not require withholding them; it requires separating the observation from conclusions still under review.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the observation
This measured lesion is smaller than on the comparison scan.
Recognize its value
That is a useful finding to discuss.
Separate the review
The complete response assessment is still under review.
Avoid overstatement
Smaller does not by itself mean cured.
Include other evidence
The team will interpret the scan with the other relevant findings.
Keep status accurate
No treatment change has been agreed yet.
Explain a category
A response category uses defined criteria, not one adjective.
Check the summary
What would you tell someone about what is known and what is still pending?
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Smaller than
Makes a comparative observation and identifies the reference image.
By itself
Limits the conclusion supported by one finding.
Has been agreed
Distinguishes an agreed decision from a possible change under discussion.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which update is supported?

Answer and explanation

A. One measured lesion is smaller; the full assessment remains under review.

The supported statement keeps the favorable measurement separate from the unfinished overall interpretation.

2. Which claim confuses observation and classification?

Answer and explanation

D. Smaller automatically means formal partial response.

Formal response categories require their applicable criteria and the full relevant assessment.

3. Which treatment statement is accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. No change has been agreed.

The case explicitly leaves any treatment change undecided.

4. Why avoid equating complete response with cure?

Answer and explanation

B. They describe different claims about disease and future outcome.

A response category does not automatically establish a permanent individual outcome.

Check explanations in the book, page 95 ↗

E Conversations

Smaller is useful, but not the whole conclusion

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Pat: Patient / Dr Ng: Oncologist

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • Clinical response
  • radiological response
  • comparison scan
  • Non-target lesions
  • partial response
  • target lesion
  • complete response
  • assessment criteria
  • response assessment
  • Remission
  1. Pat
    The report says the spot is smaller. I have been waiting to hear whether I can call that good news.
  2. Dr Ng
    The 1 shows the reference point. This measured lesion is smaller, and that is useful information.
  3. Pat
    I would like to understand the finding without saying more than the report can actually support.
  4. Dr Ng
    That is sensible. The full 2 is still under review; one measurement is not the entire conclusion.
  5. Pat
    Does smaller automatically mean the treatment has produced what the team calls a partial response?
  6. Dr Ng
    A 3 has defined criteria. I need the full assessment before using that label for your results.
  7. Pat
    I noticed the report described other findings as well. Are they part of the same review?
  8. Dr Ng
    Yes. A 4 is selected for measurement, but other findings can matter to the overall interpretation.
  9. Pat
    Then I should not assume that measuring one selected area means every other area has been ignored.
  10. Dr Ng
    Correct. 5 and any new findings may need assessment under the appropriate system.
  11. Pat
    My symptoms have also changed. Should I describe those even if the scan appears encouraging?
  12. Dr Ng
    Absolutely. 6 and imaging findings offer different information; we need the clinical context too.
  13. Pat
    I want to tell my family there is an encouraging change, but not that everything has disappeared.
  14. Dr Ng
    That is accurate. A 7 has not been established by the facts we are discussing.
  15. Pat
    And even that term would need explaining rather than being used as a simple synonym for cure?
  16. Dr Ng
    Yes. 8 and cure are not interchangeable promises about an individual's future.
  17. Pat
    Does the smaller measurement mean you have already decided to change the treatment schedule?
  18. Dr Ng
    No. We must finish the review using the relevant 9 and clinical context before discussing any recommendation.
  19. Pat
    Then my summary is that one lesion is smaller, the full review is pending, and the plan has not changed yet.
  20. Dr Ng
    Exactly. We will explain the 10 and its implications when the complete assessment is available.

Answers and explanations
  1. comparison scan Turn 2. A comparison scan provides the earlier imaging reference for the reported change in the measured lesion.
  2. response assessment Turn 4. Response assessment considers the relevant evidence and criteria rather than assigning a category from one descriptive word.
  3. partial response Turn 6. A partial response must meet the applicable assessment criteria and cannot be inferred from the word smaller alone.
  4. target lesion Turn 8. A target lesion is selected for measurement under an assessment system and does not represent every relevant disease finding.
  5. Non-target lesions Turn 10. Non-target lesions are relevant disease findings assessed outside the selected target measurements and may affect interpretation.
  6. Clinical response Turn 12. Clinical response concerns changes in symptoms, signs, or functioning and adds information distinct from imaging measurements.
  7. complete response Turn 14. Complete response refers to disappearance of detectable findings under applicable criteria and has not been established here.
  8. Remission Turn 16. Remission describes reduction or disappearance of disease signs and should not be used as an automatic guarantee of permanent cure.
  9. assessment criteria Turn 18. Assessment criteria are the defined rules used to classify changes and support interpretation rather than an automatic schedule change.
  10. radiological response Turn 20. Radiological response is change assessed on imaging and will be discussed with the full clinical picture when the review is complete.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Pat: The report says the spot is smaller. I have been waiting to hear whether I can call that good news.
  2. Dr Ng: The comparison scan shows the reference point. This measured lesion is smaller, and that is useful information.
  3. Pat: I would like to understand the finding without saying more than the report can actually support.
  4. Dr Ng: That is sensible. The full response assessment is still under review; one measurement is not the entire conclusion.
  5. Pat: Does smaller automatically mean the treatment has produced what the team calls a partial response?
  6. Dr Ng: A partial response has defined criteria. I need the full assessment before using that label for your results.
  7. Pat: I noticed the report described other findings as well. Are they part of the same review?
  8. Dr Ng: Yes. A target lesion is selected for measurement, but other findings can matter to the overall interpretation.
  9. Pat: Then I should not assume that measuring one selected area means every other area has been ignored.
  10. Dr Ng: Correct. Non-target lesions and any new findings may need assessment under the appropriate system.
  11. Pat: My symptoms have also changed. Should I describe those even if the scan appears encouraging?
  12. Dr Ng: Absolutely. Clinical response and imaging findings offer different information; we need the clinical context too.
  13. Pat: I want to tell my family there is an encouraging change, but not that everything has disappeared.
  14. Dr Ng: That is accurate. A complete response has not been established by the facts we are discussing.
  15. Pat: And even that term would need explaining rather than being used as a simple synonym for cure?
  16. Dr Ng: Yes. Remission and cure are not interchangeable promises about an individual's future.
  17. Pat: Does the smaller measurement mean you have already decided to change the treatment schedule?
  18. Dr Ng: No. We must finish the review using the relevant assessment criteria and clinical context before discussing any recommendation.
  19. Pat: Then my summary is that one lesion is smaller, the full review is pending, and the plan has not changed yet.
  20. Dr Ng: Exactly. We will explain the radiological response and its implications when the complete assessment is available.
Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Smaller is not automatically a response category

A patient reads a scan comparison.

  1. Patient: The report says a measured lesion is smaller. Can I call that a partial response?
  2. Oncologist: A response category requires the relevant criteria and complete assessment, not one phrase alone.
  3. Patient: I am glad about the change, but I do not want to overstate what it means.
  4. Oncologist: We can acknowledge the finding while explaining what the overall review establishes.
  5. Patient: Does a smaller measurement also guarantee that every area of disease has improved?
  6. Oncologist: No. The actual comparison and other relevant findings must be reviewed.
  7. Patient: Could differences in technique or the comparison study affect how a change is interpreted?
  8. Oncologist: They can matter, which is why the report and clinical context belong together.
  9. Patient: Then the measurement, response category, and treatment decision remain separate questions.
  10. Oncologist: Exactly. We will explain the actual conclusion without turning a favorable detail into a guarantee.
Stable disease still needs explanation

A patient asks about a response term.

  1. Oncologist: You asked what stable disease means in the reviewed assessment.
  2. Patient: I heard stable and thought it meant nothing clinically important had happened.
  3. Oncologist: The term needs the actual criteria and context; it is not a judgment that the treatment was pointless.
  4. Patient: Could we distinguish a response category from how I feel day to day?
  5. Oncologist: Yes. Symptoms, function, imaging, and the relevant assessment may describe different aspects of care.
  6. Patient: I would like to know what the category supports and what it does not decide by itself.
  7. Oncologist: We should explain that before discussing the actual next treatment decision.
  8. Patient: Does the word stable guarantee that the situation cannot change before the next review?
  9. Oncologist: No. It describes the assessed state, not a promise about every future event.
  10. Patient: That helps me understand the term without treating it as either failure or permanent reassurance.
A comparison report lacks the earlier images

Two clinicians discuss an incomplete comparison.

  1. Radiologist: The earlier written report is available, but I do not yet have the actual comparison images.
  2. Oncologist: Then we should not describe the image-to-image comparison as complete.
  3. Radiologist: I can state the limitation and explain what additional material would support the review.
  4. Oncologist: Please keep that limitation visible rather than shortening the report to a definite response category.
  5. Radiologist: The patient may ask whether the delay itself means an unfavorable finding.
  6. Oncologist: We should explain that missing comparison material is not a clinical outcome.
  7. Radiologist: Once the images arrive, the appropriate review can address the actual comparison question.
  8. Oncologist: Agreed. We should identify who follows up the missing material and communicates the reviewed result.
  9. Radiologist: A requested comparison is not the same as a completed one.
  10. Oncologist: That distinction belongs in both the clinical record and the patient explanation.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Smaller is useful, but not the whole conclusion
  1. Read the corrected scan discussion. Keep the encouraging observation separate from a formal response category.
  2. Swap roles. Repeat Pat's final summary without declaring cure or changing treatment.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A new finding awaiting interpretation

A scan mentions a new finding. The oncology review is incomplete. No progression category or treatment change has been agreed.

Answer and explanation

B. new

New describes the observation supplied, not its established significance.

2. Clinician: "The review remains ___."

Answer and explanation

C. incomplete

The oncology interpretation has not yet been completed in this scenario.

3. Patient: "Progression is not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

A. classified

The case does not supply a formal progression category.

4. Clinician: "No treatment change is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. agreed

A new observation does not by itself establish an agreed treatment change.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

examinations. / restaging: Reassessment of disease extent after treatment or a change. / assessment criteria: Defined rules used to classify findings or changes. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected scan discussion. Keep the encouraging observation separate from a formal response category. Swap roles. Repeat Pat's final summary without declaring cure or changing treatment. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Pat: Patient / Dr Ng: Oncologist
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A scan mentions a new finding. The oncology review is incomplete. No progression category or treatment change has been agreed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Pat: Does the smaller measurement mean you have already decided to change the treatment schedule?
Dr Ng: No. We must finish the review using the relevant assessment criteria and clinical context before discussing any recommendation.
Pat: Then my summary is that one lesion is smaller, the full review is pending, and the plan has not changed yet.
Dr Ng: Exactly. We will explain the radiological response and its implications when the complete assessment is available.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.
