Lesson 06: Discussing a response-assessment scan
Original fictional case: Pat's report describes a measured lesion as smaller than on the comparison scan. The complete response assessment is still under oncology review, and no formal response category or treatment change has been agreed. Dr Ng must acknowledge the encouraging observation without declaring cure, complete response, or a settled next regimen.
Language workshop: Smaller is useful, but not the whole conclusion
Communication goal: Explain a scan comparison while distinguishing measurement, interpretation, and an agreed treatment decision.
Language guidance: A smaller lesion is an observation, not automatically a formal partial response. Complete response and cure are not interchangeable claims.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): This measured lesion is smaller than on the comparison scan. / That is a useful finding to discuss. / The complete response assessment is still under review. / Smaller does not by itself mean cured. / The team will interpret the scan with the other relevant findings. / No treatment change has been agreed yet. / A response category uses defined criteria, not one adjective. / What would you tell someone about what is known and what is still pending? / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: response assessment: Evaluation of how disease has changed during or after treatment. / comparison scan: Earlier imaging study used as a reference. / measurable lesion: Abnormality that can be measured under the relevant assessment criteria. / target lesion: Selected lesion followed for measurement under a response-assessment system. / non-target lesion: Disease finding assessed outside the selected target-lesion measurements. / partial response: Reduction meeting the relevant criteria without a complete response. / complete response: Disappearance of detectable disease findings under the applicable response criteria. / stable disease: Disease not meeting the relevant response or progression thresholds. / progressive disease: Worsening that meets applicable progression criteria. / new lesion: Newly identified abnormality requiring interpretation in context. / radiological response: Change in disease appearance assessed using imaging. / clinical response: Change assessed through clinical symptoms, signs, or functioning. / remission: Reduction or disappearance of signs of disease, described as partial or complete when appropriate. / recurrence: Return of cancer after a period of improvement or remission. / measurement variability: Differences arising from how measurements are obtained or interpreted. / scan interval: Time between imaging examinations. / restaging: Reassessment of disease extent after treatment or a change. / assessment criteria: Defined rules used to classify findings or changes. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected scan discussion. Keep the encouraging observation separate from a formal response category. Swap roles. Repeat Pat's final summary without declaring cure or changing treatment. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Pat: Patient / Dr Ng: Oncologist
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.

07Explaining a clinical trial invitationAn option to discuss, not an obligation+

Your goal

Explain research participation while separating eligibility, consent, and proven benefit.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

An option to discuss, not an obligation

Dr Ortiz discusses a possible treatment trial with Val. Eligibility screening is not complete, and Val has not consented or enrolled. The trial compares approaches using random assignment. No investigational product, benefit probability, or payment terms are supplied. Val worries that declining would harm the relationship with the care team.

Val: Patient / Dr Ortiz: Oncologist

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the participation status?

Answer and explanation

D. Eligibility review incomplete; no consent or enrollment

The scenario keeps eligibility, consent, and enrollment as unfinished and distinct stages.

2. How does the trial assign approaches?

Answer and explanation

B. Random assignment

The brief explicitly states random assignment without describing a guaranteed superior group.

3. What worries Val?

Answer and explanation

B. Declining might harm the care relationship

Val's concern is pressure on the relationship rather than a supplied clinical result.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Trial eligibility is not enrollment, and consent does not guarantee benefit. Randomization is not a clinician choosing the group believed best for one participant.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
clinical trial
Research study evaluating a health intervention in people.discuss a clinical trial
eligibility criteria
Requirements determining whether a person may enter a study.review eligibility criteria
screening visit
Assessment checking whether trial-entry requirements are met.arrange a screening visit
enrollment
Formal entry into a study after required processes.distinguish enrollment from screening
randomization
Assignment to study groups through a chance-based process.explain randomization
study arm
Group receiving a specified intervention or approach in a trial.describe a study arm
control group
Comparison group used to evaluate an intervention.explain the control group
investigational treatment
Treatment being studied for the relevant use.discuss an investigational treatment
standard of care
Accepted clinical approach for the relevant setting and circumstances.compare with standard of care
research protocol
Approved plan specifying how a study is conducted.explain the research protocol
primary endpoint
Main outcome used to evaluate the study question.identify the primary endpoint
overall survival
Time from a defined starting point until death from any cause.interpret overall survival
progression-free survival
Time from a defined point without disease progression or death under study definitions.explain progression-free survival
voluntary participation
Taking part by choice without improper pressure.protect voluntary participation
withdrawal
Ending participation through the applicable study process.explain withdrawal options
consent discussion
Conversation supporting an informed decision about participation.allow time for the consent discussion
research coordinator
Professional organizing study visits and participation processes.contact the research coordinator
therapeutic misconception
Mistaken belief that research is designed solely to provide individualized treatment benefit.address therapeutic misconception
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Explain withdrawal and remaining care using the actual protocol and applicable duties. Do not invent payment, access, or administrative guarantees.

C Notice the language

Keep research and care roles clear

An invitation can feel like a recommendation the patient must obey. Explain the research purpose, uncertainty, and voluntary decision without presenting an experimental option as proven superior.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Introduce research
This is an option to discuss, not an obligation.
State uncertainty
The study is asking a question that is not already settled.
Explain assignment
Random assignment determines the study group.
Separate stages
Eligibility review is not the same as enrollment.
Protect the relationship
Declining research does not remove your right to appropriate care.
Invite review
Take the opportunity to ask about visits, risks, and alternatives.
Avoid a guarantee
An investigational treatment is not proven better simply because it is new.
Clarify withdrawal
We will explain the actual withdrawal process and remaining care arrangements.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
May be eligible
Describes a possibility before the entry criteria have been checked.
Investigational
Identifies a research status, not a promise of superior treatment.
Not an obligation
Removes an implied demand while leaving the option available for discussion.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which statement separates the stages correctly?

Answer and explanation

D. Eligibility is under review; Val is not enrolled.

The case gives neither completed eligibility nor consent nor enrollment.

2. Which claim creates therapeutic misconception?

Answer and explanation

C. The research exists solely to give Val the best individual treatment.

Research evaluates a question and cannot be presented as a guarantee of individualized benefit.

3. What does randomization mean?

Answer and explanation

B. Chance-based assignment under the study process

Randomization assigns groups through chance rather than individualized selection of a supposedly superior group.

4. Which response to Val's concern is appropriate?

Answer and explanation

B. Declining research does not remove the right to appropriate care.

Voluntary participation requires avoiding coercion and clarifying continuing care.

An option to discuss, not an obligation

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Val: Patient / Dr Ortiz: Oncologist

  1. Val
    You mentioned a trial. I worry that saying no would make you think I am not cooperating with care.
  2. Dr Ortiz
    This 1 is an option to discuss. Declining research does not remove your right to appropriate care.
  3. Val
    Am I already accepted because you brought it up, or is there another process before that?
  4. Dr Ortiz
    We still need to review the 2. Discussing the study does not establish that you can enter it.
  5. Val
    I would like to understand what the study is trying to find out before considering any forms.
  6. Dr Ortiz
    The 3 describes its question, procedures, and participant requirements. We will review the relevant details with you.
  7. Val
    Would you choose whichever treatment group you think is best for me after I join?
  8. Dr Ortiz
    This study uses 4. Assignment follows a chance-based process rather than my choosing an arm for you.
  9. Val
    That matters to my decision. I do not want to assume that the new option is already known to be better.
  10. Dr Ortiz
    An 5 is being studied; its possible advantages and disadvantages are not a promised result for you.
  11. Val
    Please compare participation with the ordinary care options I could discuss outside the study.
  12. Dr Ortiz
    We should explain the relevant 6 and other reasonable alternatives, not present research as the only possible conversation.
  13. Val
    The visits and tests may affect my work. Can I ask about those before deciding whether to proceed?
  14. Dr Ortiz
    Certainly. The 7 can help explain practical arrangements, alongside our discussion of the clinical questions.
  15. Val
    If I first agree and later want to stop, I need to understand what happens to my care.
  16. Dr Ortiz
    We will explain 8 using the actual study process and clarify the remaining care arrangements.

    I would like time to read the information and return with questions rather than sign because I feel rushed.
  18. Dr Ortiz
    That is part of a proper 9. Questions and time to understand are important to a voluntary decision.
  19. Val
    Then eligibility is still being checked, and I have not consented or joined by having this conversation.
  20. Dr Ortiz
    Correct. 10 has not happened. We can continue discussing the option without treating today as a commitment.

Answers and explanations
  1. clinical trial Turn 2. A clinical trial is research evaluating an intervention, and an invitation to discuss it is not an obligation to join.
  2. eligibility criteria Turn 4. Eligibility criteria are entry requirements that remain under review and do not establish enrollment by themselves.
  3. research protocol Turn 6. The research protocol sets out the study's purpose and procedures and should guide an accurate explanation of participation.
  4. randomization Turn 8. Randomization assigns study groups through a chance-based process rather than the clinician selecting an individually preferred arm.
  5. investigational treatment Turn 10. An investigational treatment is being studied for the relevant use and is not proven superior merely because it is new.
  6. standard of care Turn 12. Standard of care describes accepted clinical approaches in context and belongs in the discussion of alternatives to research participation.
  7. research coordinator Turn 14. A research coordinator can clarify study arrangements and visits while clinical and consent questions remain with the appropriate professionals.
  8. withdrawal Turn 16. Withdrawal is ending participation through the applicable process, with its details and continuing care discussed accurately.
  9. consent discussion Turn 18. A consent discussion supports understanding and a voluntary decision rather than treating a signature as the whole process.
  10. Enrollment Turn 20. Enrollment is formal entry after the required study processes and has not occurred in this scenario.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Val: You mentioned a trial. I worry that saying no would make you think I am not cooperating with care.
  2. Dr Ortiz: This clinical trial is an option to discuss. Declining research does not remove your right to appropriate care.
  3. Val: Am I already accepted because you brought it up, or is there another process before that?
  4. Dr Ortiz: We still need to review the eligibility criteria. Discussing the study does not establish that you can enter it.
  5. Val: I would like to understand what the study is trying to find out before considering any forms.
  6. Dr Ortiz: The research protocol describes its question, procedures, and participant requirements. We will review the relevant details with you.
  7. Val: Would you choose whichever treatment group you think is best for me after I join?
  8. Dr Ortiz: This study uses randomization. Assignment follows a chance-based process rather than my choosing an arm for you.
  9. Val: That matters to my decision. I do not want to assume that the new option is already known to be better.
  10. Dr Ortiz: An investigational treatment is being studied; its possible advantages and disadvantages are not a promised result for you.
  11. Val: Please compare participation with the ordinary care options I could discuss outside the study.
  12. Dr Ortiz: We should explain the relevant standard of care and other reasonable alternatives, not present research as the only possible conversation.
  13. Val: The visits and tests may affect my work. Can I ask about those before deciding whether to proceed?
  14. Dr Ortiz: Certainly. The research coordinator can help explain practical arrangements, alongside our discussion of the clinical questions.
  15. Val: If I first agree and later want to stop, I need to understand what happens to my care.
  16. Dr Ortiz: We will explain withdrawal using the actual study process and clarify the remaining care arrangements.
  17. Val: I would like time to read the information and return with questions rather than sign because I feel rushed.
  18. Dr Ortiz: That is part of a proper consent discussion. Questions and time to understand are important to a voluntary decision.
  19. Val: Then eligibility is still being checked, and I have not consented or joined by having this conversation.
  20. Dr Ortiz: Correct. Enrollment has not happened. We can continue discussing the option without treating today as a commitment.
Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A trial invitation is not enrollment

A patient asks about joining a study.

  1. Patient: I received an invitation to discuss a trial. Does that mean I am already enrolled?
  2. Oncologist: No. An invitation, eligibility review, consent, and enrollment are different steps.
  3. Patient: I want to understand the study before anyone assumes I have agreed to participate.
  4. Oncologist: The relevant information and questions belong before the actual consent decision.
  5. Patient: Does being invited guarantee that I meet every eligibility criterion?
  6. Oncologist: It does not. The study team must review the actual criteria and your circumstances.
  7. Patient: I also want to know what happens to ordinary care if I decide not to join.
  8. Oncologist: That should be explained clearly using the actual study and care arrangements.
  9. Patient: Then a discussion can help me decide without making the decision for me.
  10. Oncologist: Exactly. Participation should not be inferred from accepting information or attending a meeting.
Randomization is not a personalized recommendation

A research clinician explains allocation.

  1. Research clinician: You asked whether randomization means the team chooses the arm it thinks will work best for you.
  2. Patient: That was my assumption, because I thought every assignment was individually selected.
  3. Research clinician: Randomization allocates according to the study method rather than that kind of personal choice.
  4. Patient: Could you explain the actual allocation process and what I would know about it?
  5. Research clinician: Yes. We need to use the real protocol, including any masking and relevant alternatives.
  6. Patient: Does joining guarantee that the experimental approach will be better than the comparison?
  7. Research clinician: No. The study addresses uncertainty, and benefit cannot be promised in advance.
  8. Patient: I would like those limits explained before I decide whether participation fits my priorities.
  9. Research clinician: That is part of the informed discussion, alongside risks, burdens, and your questions.
  10. Patient: Then I can distinguish research allocation from a clinician's individualized recommendation.
Withdrawing needs an actual explanation

A participant asks about leaving a study.

  1. Patient: If I choose to leave the study, does everything connected with it disappear immediately?
  2. Research clinician: We should explain withdrawal using the actual protocol and applicable rules, not a blanket promise.
  3. Patient: I want to understand the difference between stopping study treatment and later follow-up.
  4. Research clinician: Those may involve different arrangements, which need to be explained clearly.
  5. Patient: What about information already collected before I withdraw?
  6. Research clinician: Its handling depends on the actual consent documents and relevant requirements.
  7. Patient: I do not want someone to discourage my question by saying that joining is irreversible.
  8. Research clinician: Your questions and voluntary participation matter; we should explain the available choices accurately.
  9. Patient: Could we review the relevant document together before I decide what to request?
  10. Research clinician: Yes. We will distinguish the decisions and consequences without inventing a universal withdrawal rule.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

An option to discuss, not an obligation
  1. Read the corrected research discussion. Contrast invitation, eligibility, consent, and enrollment.
  2. Swap roles. Repeat the randomization explanation and the response to perceived pressure.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Answer and explanation

The case explicitly states that formal study entry has not occurred.

Answer and explanation

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. REFERENCE
Course: Oncology English
Professional audience: oncologists, medical oncologists, radiation oncologists, surgical oncologists, oncology fellows
Scope: All people, records, figures, and encounters are fictional. This is professional English practice, not medical advice, clinical training, a treatment protocol, or certification. Use current evidence, authorized supervision where required, local law, and your actual clinical and emergency procedures. Do not use an exercise to decide that symptoms can safely wait, select treatment, or calculate a dose. Communication about privacy, consent, and professional duties depends on jurisdiction and setting. Use qualified language support and protect real patient information.
Lesson 07: Explaining a clinical trial invitation
Original fictional case: Dr Ortiz discusses a possible treatment trial with Val. Eligibility screening is not complete, and Val has not consented or enrolled. The trial compares approaches using random assignment. No investigational product, benefit probability, or payment terms are supplied. Val worries that declining would harm the relationship with the care team.
Language workshop: An option to discuss, not an obligation
Communication goal: Explain research participation while separating eligibility, consent, and proven benefit.
Language guidance: Trial eligibility is not enrollment, and consent does not guarantee benefit. Randomization is not a clinician choosing the group believed best for one participant.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): This is an option to discuss, not an obligation. / The study is asking a question that is not already settled. / Random assignment determines the study group. / Eligibility review is not the same as enrollment. / Declining research does not remove your right to appropriate care. / Take the opportunity to ask about visits, risks, and alternatives. / An investigational treatment is not proven better simply because it is new. / We will explain the actual withdrawal process and remaining care arrangements. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: clinical trial: Research study evaluating a health intervention in people. / eligibility criteria: Requirements determining whether a person may enter a study. / screening visit: Assessment checking whether trial-entry requirements are met. / enrollment: Formal entry into a study after required processes. / randomization: Assignment to study groups through a chance-based process. / study arm: Group receiving a specified intervention or approach in a trial. / control group: Comparison group used to evaluate an intervention. / investigational treatment: Treatment being studied for the relevant use. / standard of care: Accepted clinical approach for the relevant setting and circumstances. / research protocol: Approved plan specifying how a study is conducted. / primary endpoint: Main outcome used to evaluate the study question. / overall survival: Time from a defined starting point until death from any cause. / progression-free survival: Time from a defined point without disease progression or death under study definitions. / voluntary participation: Taking part by choice without improper pressure. / withdrawal: Ending participation through the applicable study process. / consent discussion: Conversation supporting an informed decision about participation. / research coordinator: Professional organizing study visits and participation processes. / therapeutic misconception: Mistaken belief that research is designed solely to provide individualized treatment benefit. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected research discussion. Contrast invitation, eligibility, consent, and enrollment. Swap roles. Repeat the randomization explanation and the response to perceived pressure. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Val: Patient / Dr Ortiz: Oncologist
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Jo has agreed to a trial-screening visit. Eligibility remains unknown. Jo has not enrolled and wants the withdrawal information explained before making a participation decision.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

08Discussing palliative care and prioritiesSupport alongside treatment+

Your goal

Explain a supportive-care referral and elicit goals without equating it with abandonment or an automatic change in treatment.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

Support alongside treatment

Dr Ahmed offers Rowan a palliative-care consultation for symptoms and distress while an anticancer-treatment discussion continues. Rowan hears the referral as a decision to stop treatment. No decision to stop treatment, enter hospice, or set a resuscitation status is supplied. Rowan wants more comfortable time with family and clearer help with symptoms.

Rowan: Patient / Dr Ahmed: Oncologist

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Why is the consultation offered?

Answer and explanation

B. Symptoms and distress

The referral addresses symptoms and distress while the treatment discussion continues.

2. What does Rowan fear?

Answer and explanation

B. That treatment is being stopped

Rowan interprets palliative care as a decision to end anticancer treatment.

3. Which preference is stated?

Answer and explanation

A. More comfortable time with family

Rowan names comfort and family time, not a completed decision about other care.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Palliative care can accompany anticancer treatment. A referral does not automatically mean hospice enrollment, treatment cessation, or a resuscitation decision.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
palliative care
Care addressing symptoms, distress, and quality of life in serious illness.introduce palliative care
hospice care
Care focused on comfort near the end of life under applicable service criteria.distinguish hospice care
symptom management
Assessment and treatment of distressing symptoms.coordinate symptom management
goals-of-care discussion
Conversation about priorities and how care should respond to them.open a goals-of-care discussion
serious-illness conversation
Discussion of understanding, concerns, values, and care in serious illness.structure a serious-illness conversation
advance care planning
Process of discussing and recording preferences for future care.offer advance care planning
advance directive
Document expressing future-care wishes or decision arrangements under applicable law.review an advance directive
surrogate decision-maker
Person authorized to decide when the patient cannot under applicable rules.clarify the surrogate decision-maker
resuscitation status
Documented decision or order about resuscitation in the relevant setting.clarify resuscitation status
comfort-focused care
Care organized primarily around comfort and relief of distress.explain comfort-focused care
concurrent care
Care approaches provided alongside one another.discuss concurrent care
care preference
Person's expressed wishes about aspects of care.elicit a care preference
family meeting
Planned discussion involving the patient and appropriate family or support people.arrange a family meeting
caregiver strain
Physical or emotional burden experienced by someone providing care.acknowledge caregiver strain
spiritual concern
Concern about meaning, belief, connection, or existential distress.invite a spiritual concern
symptom burden
Combined effect of symptoms on the person's life.assess symptom burden
interdisciplinary support
Help provided by professionals with complementary care roles.coordinate interdisciplinary support
values clarification
Exploration of what matters to a person in a care decision.support values clarification
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

A family member's presence does not automatically establish decision-making authority. Discuss preferences and legal arrangements accurately for the setting.

C Notice the language

Explore the meaning the patient heard

Terms associated with serious illness can carry meanings beyond their clinical definition. Ask what the referral sounded like before correcting the misunderstanding.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Ask what was heard
What did the phrase palliative care suggest to you?
Correct the inference
This referral is not a decision to stop treatment.
Explain the purpose
The team can help with symptoms and distress alongside other care.
Elicit a priority
What would make the coming weeks more manageable for you?
Separate decisions
Hospice and resuscitation decisions require their own discussions.
Invite support
We can discuss who you want involved in a family meeting.
Avoid abandonment
We will continue to address your care needs.
Make goals concrete
More comfortable time with family is a priority we should keep visible.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Alongside
Describes concurrent care rather than replacement or abandonment.
Not a decision to
Corrects an inference about an action that has not been agreed.
What would make
Invites a concrete priority rather than assuming the clinician knows it already.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which statement explains the referral correctly?

Answer and explanation

A. Palliative care can support symptoms alongside treatment.

Palliative support can accompany other treatment and does not settle separate care decisions.

2. Which question explores the misunderstanding?

Answer and explanation

D. What did the phrase suggest to you?

Asking what the phrase suggested invites Rowan's interpretation without blame or a leading conclusion.

3. What priority should remain visible?

Answer and explanation

A. Comfortable time with family

Comfortable family time is Rowan's explicit priority in this fictional conversation.

4. Which assumption is unsafe?

Answer and explanation

D. Every relative present has automatic decision-making authority.

Presence or relationship alone does not establish authority under the relevant legal arrangements.

E Conversations

Support alongside treatment

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Rowan: Patient / Dr Ahmed: Oncologist

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • symptom burden
  • concurrent care
  • family meeting
  • Caregiver strain
  • Hospice care
  • surrogate decision-maker
  • palliative care
  • resuscitation status
  • goals-of-care discussion
  • symptom management
  1. Rowan
    When you mentioned palliative care, I heard that you were giving up on the cancer treatment.
  2. Dr Ahmed
    What did 1 suggest to you? I want to understand that concern before explaining the referral.
  3. Rowan
    I thought it meant every other treatment would stop, and that I would no longer see this team.
  4. Dr Ahmed
    This is an offer of 2. It is not a decision to stop treatment or to stop addressing your care needs.
  5. Rowan
    Then please tell me what the additional team would actually help with in everyday life.
  6. Dr Ahmed
    They can help with 3, distress, and the effects of serious illness on you and those supporting you.
  7. Rowan
    I want more comfortable time with my family. At present the symptoms take over the entire day.
  8. Dr Ahmed
    That 4 matters. We should keep comfortable family time visible when we discuss care options.
  9. Rowan
    Does agreeing to this consultation mean I have agreed to hospice without realizing it?
  10. Dr Ahmed
    No. 5 is a separate discussion with its own circumstances and service criteria.
  11. Rowan
    I also do not want one referral to become a decision about resuscitation without anyone asking me.
  12. Dr Ahmed
    It does not establish your 6. Those decisions require their own appropriate discussion and documentation.
  13. Rowan
    My daughter helps a great deal, but I do not want everyone to assume she makes all decisions for me.
  14. Dr Ahmed
    We should clarify that. A 7 has a specific role under the applicable arrangements, not simply because a relative attends.
  15. Rowan
    Could we have a conversation together where she can hear what I want without taking over the discussion?
  16. Dr Ahmed
    We can discuss a 8 and who you want involved, keeping your priorities and permissions clear.
  17. Rowan
    I also want someone to notice how exhausted she is. She keeps saying she is fine.
  18. Dr Ahmed
    9 is worth discussing, with her involvement and the appropriate support.
  19. Rowan
    I understand better now. The referral adds symptom support; it does not silently settle every future decision.
  20. Dr Ahmed
    Exactly. A 10 keeps those priorities explicit while we continue the other necessary clinical conversations.

Answers and explanations
  1. palliative care Turn 2. Palliative care addresses symptoms and quality of life in serious illness and can accompany anticancer treatment.
  2. concurrent care Turn 4. Concurrent care means approaches can be provided alongside one another, rather than assuming one automatically replaces another.
  3. symptom management Turn 6. Symptom management addresses distressing symptoms and is one reason for this consultation while other treatment discussions continue.
  4. symptom burden Turn 8. Symptom burden is the combined effect of symptoms on daily life and helps make Rowan's stated priority concrete.
  5. Hospice care Turn 10. Hospice care has a distinct comfort-focused purpose and applicable service criteria; it is not automatically established by this referral.
  6. resuscitation status Turn 12. Resuscitation status is a separate documented care decision and is not determined by accepting a palliative-care consultation.
  7. surrogate decision-maker Turn 14. A surrogate decision-maker acts under applicable authority when required, which is not established merely by being a helpful relative.
  8. family meeting Turn 16. A family meeting is a planned conversation with the appropriate people, guided by the patient's wishes and information-sharing arrangements.
  9. Caregiver strain Turn 18. Caregiver strain describes the burden on someone providing care and can be addressed without replacing the patient's own voice.
  10. goals-of-care discussion Turn 20. A goals-of-care discussion explores priorities and appropriate care rather than automatically ending treatment or assigning a resuscitation status.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Rowan: When you mentioned palliative care, I heard that you were giving up on the cancer treatment.
  2. Dr Ahmed: What did palliative care suggest to you? I want to understand that concern before explaining the referral.
  3. Rowan: I thought it meant every other treatment would stop, and that I would no longer see this team.
  4. Dr Ahmed: This is an offer of concurrent care. It is not a decision to stop treatment or to stop addressing your care needs.
  5. Rowan: Then please tell me what the additional team would actually help with in everyday life.
  6. Dr Ahmed: They can help with symptom management, distress, and the effects of serious illness on you and those supporting you.
  7. Rowan: I want more comfortable time with my family. At present the symptoms take over the entire day.
  8. Dr Ahmed: That symptom burden matters. We should keep comfortable family time visible when we discuss care options.
  9. Rowan: Does agreeing to this consultation mean I have agreed to hospice without realizing it?
  10. Dr Ahmed: No. Hospice care is a separate discussion with its own circumstances and service criteria.
  11. Rowan: I also do not want one referral to become a decision about resuscitation without anyone asking me.
  12. Dr Ahmed: It does not establish your resuscitation status. Those decisions require their own appropriate discussion and documentation.
  13. Rowan: My daughter helps a great deal, but I do not want everyone to assume she makes all decisions for me.
  14. Dr Ahmed: We should clarify that. A surrogate decision-maker has a specific role under the applicable arrangements, not simply because a relative attends.
  15. Rowan: Could we have a conversation together where she can hear what I want without taking over the discussion?
  16. Dr Ahmed: We can discuss a family meeting and who you want involved, keeping your priorities and permissions clear.
  17. Rowan: I also want someone to notice how exhausted she is. She keeps saying she is fine.
  18. Dr Ahmed: Caregiver strain is worth discussing, with her involvement and the appropriate support.
  19. Rowan: I understand better now. The referral adds symptom support; it does not silently settle every future decision.
  20. Dr Ahmed: Exactly. A goals-of-care discussion keeps those priorities explicit while we continue the other necessary clinical conversations.
Dialogue in the book, page 73 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Palliative care alongside active treatment

A patient asks whether support means treatment is ending.

  1. Patient: The palliative-care referral frightened me because I assumed it meant every cancer treatment was stopping.
  2. Oncologist: Palliative care can address symptoms and support alongside disease-directed care; the referral does not automatically stop treatment.
  3. Patient: I would like the actual purpose of this referral explained for my situation.
  4. Oncologist: We should identify the symptoms, practical needs, and questions the team can help address.
  5. Patient: Does it automatically mean that I have entered hospice care?
  6. Oncologist: No. Those terms and services are not interchangeable, and the actual arrangements need explanation.
  7. Patient: I also do not want a resuscitation decision inferred from accepting symptom support.
  8. Oncologist: That would be a separate discussion under the actual clinical and legal process.
  9. Patient: Then I can consider the support without agreeing to decisions that were never discussed.
  10. Oncologist: Exactly. Clear distinctions let you understand what the referral does and does not mean.
A family request differs from the patient's wish

A clinician clarifies whose preference is being expressed.

  1. Relative: I think the patient should avoid hearing difficult details because they might lose hope.
  2. Oncologist: I hear your concern, but we need to understand the patient's own information preferences.
  3. Relative: Can you assume that I know best because I attend most appointments?
  4. Oncologist: Your support is important, but attendance does not automatically determine what the patient wants disclosed.
  5. Relative: How can we discuss this without making the patient feel abandoned by the family?
  6. Oncologist: We can ask respectfully about preferred information, support, and the appropriate decision-making arrangements.
  7. Relative: I do not want my worry recorded as though it were the patient's stated choice.
  8. Oncologist: That distinction matters. We should attribute your concern to you rather than merge the accounts.
  9. Relative: Then the conversation can include the family without replacing the patient's voice.
  10. Oncologist: Yes. We will follow the actual preferences, capacity assessment, and applicable responsibilities.
Symptom priorities change over time

A patient revisits the focus of supportive care.

  1. Patient: At our last visit, pain was my main concern. Today fatigue is affecting me more.
  2. Palliative clinician: Thank you for updating the priority. The earlier note should not freeze the agenda.
  3. Patient: I want the team to understand the impact on ordinary tasks, not just hear a symptom label.
  4. Palliative clinician: Please describe what has become harder and how the pattern has changed.
  5. Patient: Does changing the priority mean the earlier pain concern no longer matters?
  6. Palliative clinician: Not necessarily. We can distinguish improvement, persistence, and a new leading concern.
  7. Patient: I would like the actual care options explained after the appropriate assessment.
  8. Palliative clinician: That discussion should use your current situation rather than an unchanged template.
  9. Patient: Could we confirm who coordinates the follow-up with the oncology team?
  10. Palliative clinician: Yes. Shared care needs clear responsibility and communication, not an assumption that another service has everything.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Support alongside treatment
  1. Read the corrected supportive-care conversation. Stress alongside and separate discussion.
  2. Swap roles. Repeat the response to Rowan's fear without promising an outcome or assigning a resuscitation status.

Name a concrete care priority

Taylor wants symptom support while treatment options are reviewed. Taylor would like friend Jo at a meeting but has not named Jo as a legally authorized decision-maker.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Taylor: "I want help with my ___."

Answer and explanation

D. symptoms

Symptom support is the care need Taylor explicitly identifies.

2. Clinician: "Treatment options remain under ___."

Answer and explanation

A. review

The options are still being reviewed and no treatment decision is supplied.

3. Taylor: "I would like ___ at the meeting."

Answer and explanation

B. Jo

Jo is the friend Taylor wishes to include in the discussion.

4. Clinician: "Attendance does not establish decision-making ___."

Answer and explanation

D. authority

Being invited to a meeting does not automatically create legal decision-making authority.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. REFERENCE
Course: Oncology English
Professional audience: oncologists, medical oncologists, radiation oncologists, surgical oncologists, oncology fellows
Scope: All people, records, figures, and encounters are fictional. This is professional English practice, not medical advice, clinical training, a treatment protocol, or certification. Lesson 08: Discussing palliative care and priorities
Original fictional case: Dr Ahmed offers Rowan a palliative-care consultation for symptoms and distress while an anticancer-treatment discussion continues. Rowan hears the referral as a decision to stop treatment. No decision to stop treatment, enter hospice, or set a resuscitation status is supplied. Rowan wants more comfortable time with family and clearer help with symptoms.
Language workshop: Support alongside treatment
Communication goal: Explain a supportive-care referral and elicit goals without equating it with abandonment or an automatic change in treatment.
Language guidance: Palliative care can accompany anticancer treatment. A referral does not automatically mean hospice enrollment, treatment cessation, or a resuscitation decision.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): What did the phrase palliative care suggest to you? / This referral is not a decision to stop treatment. / The team can help with symptoms and distress alongside other care. / What would make the coming weeks more manageable for you? / Hospice and resuscitation decisions require their own discussions. / We can discuss who you want involved in a family meeting. / We will continue to address your care needs. / More comfortable time with family is a priority we should keep visible. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: palliative care: Care addressing symptoms, distress, and quality of life in serious illness. / hospice care: Care focused on comfort near the end of life under applicable service criteria. / symptom management: Assessment and treatment of distressing symptoms. / goals-of-care discussion: Conversation about priorities and how care should respond to them. / serious-illness conversation: Discussion of understanding, concerns, values, and care in serious illness. / advance care planning: Process of discussing and recording preferences for future care. / advance directive: Document expressing future-care wishes or decision arrangements under applicable law. / surrogate decision-maker: Person authorized to decide when the patient cannot under applicable rules. / resuscitation status: Documented decision or order about resuscitation in the relevant setting. / comfort-focused care: Care organized primarily around comfort and relief of distress. / concurrent care: Care approaches provided alongside one another. / care preference: Person's expressed wishes about aspects of care. / family meeting: Planned discussion involving the patient and appropriate family or support people. / caregiver strain: Physical or emotional burden experienced by someone providing care. / spiritual concern: Concern about meaning, belief, connection, or existential distress. / symptom burden: Combined effect of symptoms on the person's life. / interdisciplinary support: Help provided by professionals with complementary care roles. / values clarification: Exploration of what matters to a person in a care decision. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected supportive-care conversation. Stress alongside and separate discussion. Swap roles. Repeat the response to Rowan's fear without promising an outcome or assigning a resuscitation status. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Rowan: Patient / Dr Ahmed: Oncologist
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Taylor wants symptom support while treatment options are reviewed. Taylor would like friend Jo at a meeting but has not named Jo as a legally authorized decision-maker.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Rowan: I also want someone to notice how exhausted she is. She keeps saying she is fine.
Dr Ahmed: Caregiver strain is worth discussing, with her involvement and the appropriate support.
Rowan: I understand better now. The referral adds symptom support; it does not silently settle every future decision.
Dr Ahmed: Exactly. A goals-of-care discussion keeps those priorities explicit while we continue the other necessary clinical conversations.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

More situations, different skills

Three additional workplace scenarios

Preparing a second-opinion record

Support a second opinion and distinguish a complete referral packet from a guaranteed recommendation.

Patient Mira asks Dr Hart for a second opinion. The receiving service requests the pathology report, relevant imaging, and treatment summary, with authorized transfer arrangements. The clinician supports the request. No new treatment recommendation or review appointment is confirmed.

Mira: Patient / Dr Hart: Oncologist

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • record-transfer authorization
  • Appointment confirmation
  • referral packet
  • treatment summary
  • pathology report
  • imaging studies
  1. Mira
    I would like a second opinion, but I worry that asking will sound as though I do not trust you.
  2. Dr Hart
    You can ask for another review. Let us discuss the information the receiving service needs and the transfer process.
  3. Mira
    They requested a pathology report. Is that different from the short diagnosis line in my appointment letter?
  4. Dr Hart
    Yes. The 1 contains the relevant findings, not merely the abbreviated label in a letter.
  5. Mira
    They also asked for the images, not only the written scan report. Why would both be requested?
  6. Dr Hart
    The reviewing team may need the actual 2 as well as the reports describing them.
  7. Mira
    I have had treatment at two sites. I do not want the second team to assume the list from one site is complete.
  8. Dr Hart
    We should verify the 3 across the relevant records, including what remains unconfirmed.
  9. Mira
    Does sending all the records mean the second team will definitely recommend something different?
  10. Dr Hart
    No. A second review may agree, differ, or need further information; we cannot promise its conclusion.
  11. Mira
    I would like to know what I am authorizing when the records are transferred.
  12. Dr Hart
    The 4 should identify the relevant information and recipient through the approved process.
  13. Mira
    If a specimen is requested for review, is that automatically included with an electronic report?
  14. Dr Hart
    Not necessarily. Any specimen or slide request has its own actual handling and transfer arrangements.
  15. Mira
    I will ask the receiving service what is still missing instead of assuming that one uploaded file completes everything.
  16. Dr Hart
    That is useful. A 5 can be incomplete even when some documents have arrived.
  17. Mira
    Who will tell me whether the review appointment is actually confirmed?
  18. Dr Hart
    We should identify the receiving contact and distinguish receipt of records from an accepted appointment.
  19. Mira
    Then I can ask for a review without treating the transfer itself as a new treatment plan.
  20. Dr Hart
    Exactly. 6 and the eventual clinical recommendation remain separate steps.

Answers and explanations
  1. pathology report Turn 4. The pathology report contains the relevant tissue-assessment findings and is more specific than an abbreviated diagnosis in an appointment letter.
  2. imaging studies Turn 6. Imaging studies include the acquired examinations used for review and are distinct from the written summaries describing them.
  3. treatment summary Turn 8. A treatment summary records relevant prior therapy and must be checked across sites rather than assumed complete from one institution's list.
  4. record-transfer authorization Turn 12. Record-transfer authorization defines the appropriate permission for sending specified information to the receiving service through the approved process.
  5. referral packet Turn 16. A referral packet is the collection of relevant documents and materials required by the receiving service and may need more than one uploaded file.
  6. Appointment confirmation Turn 20. Appointment confirmation establishes an actual scheduled review and is separate from a request being sent or records being received.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Mira: I would like a second opinion, but I worry that asking will sound as though I do not trust you.
  2. Dr Hart: You can ask for another review. Let us discuss the information the receiving service needs and the transfer process.
  3. Mira: They requested a pathology report. Is that different from the short diagnosis line in my appointment letter?
  4. Dr Hart: Yes. The pathology report contains the relevant findings, not merely the abbreviated label in a letter.
  5. Mira: They also asked for the images, not only the written scan report. Why would both be requested?
  6. Dr Hart: The reviewing team may need the actual imaging studies as well as the reports describing them.
  7. Mira: I have had treatment at two sites. I do not want the second team to assume the list from one site is complete.
  8. Dr Hart: We should verify the treatment summary across the relevant records, including what remains unconfirmed.
  9. Mira: Does sending all the records mean the second team will definitely recommend something different?
  10. Dr Hart: No. A second review may agree, differ, or need further information; we cannot promise its conclusion.
  11. Mira: I would like to know what I am authorizing when the records are transferred.
  12. Dr Hart: The record-transfer authorization should identify the relevant information and recipient through the approved process.
  13. Mira: If a specimen is requested for review, is that automatically included with an electronic report?
  14. Dr Hart: Not necessarily. Any specimen or slide request has its own actual handling and transfer arrangements.
  15. Mira: I will ask the receiving service what is still missing instead of assuming that one uploaded file completes everything.
  16. Dr Hart: That is useful. A referral packet can be incomplete even when some documents have arrived.
  17. Mira: Who will tell me whether the review appointment is actually confirmed?
  18. Dr Hart: We should identify the receiving contact and distinguish receipt of records from an accepted appointment.
  19. Mira: Then I can ask for a review without treating the transfer itself as a new treatment plan.
  20. Dr Hart: Exactly. Appointment confirmation and the eventual clinical recommendation remain separate steps.
The images have not arrived

The receiving team has the pathology report and treatment summary but not the imaging studies. No appointment is confirmed.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Patient: "The imaging studies are still ___."

Answer and explanation

D. missing

The receiving service has not yet received the actual imaging studies.

2. Clinician: "The report and summary have ___."

Answer and explanation

D. arrived

The scenario confirms receipt of those two documents only.

3. Patient: "The referral packet is not ___."

Answer and explanation

B. complete

Missing requested imaging means the packet remains incomplete.

4. Clinician: "The appointment is not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

D. confirmed

No scheduled review appointment has been confirmed in this update.

Check the answers, then read both exchanges aloud. Swap roles on the second reading.

This scenario in the book, page 76 ↗
An oncology handoff after hospital discharge

Reconcile inpatient changes with the oncology plan and assign unresolved questions.

Hospital clinician Dr Sen hands over patient Jules to oncologist Dr Ford after discharge. A medicine list was changed in hospital, but the outpatient record still shows an earlier list. The clinicians will verify the actual discharge instructions and assign follow-up. No medicine names, doses, or authority to restart treatment are supplied.

Dr Sen: Hospital clinician / Dr Ford: Oncologist

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • medication discrepancy
  • treatment hold
  • handoff acknowledgment
  • result ownership
  • discharge summary
  • reconciled plan
  1. Dr Sen
    Jules has been discharged, and the hospital medicine list differs from the earlier outpatient list. We need to reconcile them.
  2. Dr Ford
    Please identify the verified changes and their source rather than asking the patient to choose between two conflicting lists.
  3. Dr Sen
    The 1 is available, but I want to confirm that the current version reached your team.
  4. Dr Ford
    We will acknowledge receipt and review the actual instructions before presenting the earlier list as current.
  5. Dr Sen
    Some changes relate to the hospital episode, and the outpatient record has not yet been updated.
  6. Dr Ford
    That creates a 2 requiring verification. The language of the handoff must not imply that both lists are simultaneously correct.
  7. Dr Sen
    I will distinguish a documented instruction from a suggestion that still needs the responsible clinician's decision.
  8. Dr Ford
    Good. We also need the reason for each verified change, where the actual record provides it.
  9. Dr Sen
    A note mentions treatment being held, but the wording alone does not authorize restarting it today.
  10. Dr Ford
    A 3 and a restart decision are different. We need the responsible oncology assessment.
  11. Dr Sen
    The patient is worried that discharge means every cancer-care question has already been settled.
  12. Dr Ford
    Discharge does not automatically resolve the outpatient plan. We should explain what is confirmed and what remains open.
  13. Dr Sen
    One result is still pending, and its reviewing clinician needs to be identified explicitly.
  14. Dr Ford
    Let us assign 4 instead of assuming that whichever team sees it first will take responsibility.
  15. Dr Sen
    I will include the appropriate contact for questions about the hospital changes.
  16. Dr Ford
    And our team will confirm the oncology follow-up arrangements through the actual service process.
  17. Dr Sen
    The patient should receive one clear explanation once the conflicting information is resolved.
  18. Dr Ford
    A 5 needs verified instructions, not a merged list that preserves contradictions.
  19. Dr Sen
    I will send the current summary and confirm that the outstanding tasks have named owners.
  20. Dr Ford
    We will provide 6, keeping discharge, pending results, and treatment decisions distinct.

Answers and explanations
  1. discharge summary Turn 3. The discharge summary records the hospital episode and discharge instructions but must be checked against the actual current plan and medicine information.
  2. medication discrepancy Turn 6. A medication discrepancy is a conflict between medicine records or instructions that requires verification rather than a guess about which version applies.
  3. treatment hold Turn 10. A treatment hold indicates an interruption under the actual plan and does not itself specify when or whether treatment should resume.
  4. result ownership Turn 14. Result ownership identifies who must review and act on a pending result so it is not lost between inpatient and outpatient services.
  5. reconciled plan Turn 18. A reconciled plan aligns verified instructions and responsibilities after discrepancies are resolved rather than combining conflicting records without review.
  6. handoff acknowledgment Turn 20. Handoff acknowledgment confirms that the receiving team obtained the information and accepted the relevant responsibilities rather than merely receiving a file.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Dr Sen: Jules has been discharged, and the hospital medicine list differs from the earlier outpatient list. We need to reconcile them.
  2. Dr Ford: Please identify the verified changes and their source rather than asking the patient to choose between two conflicting lists.
  3. Dr Sen: The discharge summary is available, but I want to confirm that the current version reached your team.
  4. Dr Ford: We will acknowledge receipt and review the actual instructions before presenting the earlier list as current.
  5. Dr Sen: Some changes relate to the hospital episode, and the outpatient record has not yet been updated.
  6. Dr Ford: That creates a medication discrepancy requiring verification. The language of the handoff must not imply that both lists are simultaneously correct.
  7. Dr Sen: I will distinguish a documented instruction from a suggestion that still needs the responsible clinician's decision.
  8. Dr Ford: Good. We also need the reason for each verified change, where the actual record provides it.
  9. Dr Sen: A note mentions treatment being held, but the wording alone does not authorize restarting it today.
  10. Dr Ford: A treatment hold and a restart decision are different. We need the responsible oncology assessment.
  11. Dr Sen: The patient is worried that discharge means every cancer-care question has already been settled.
  12. Dr Ford: Discharge does not automatically resolve the outpatient plan. We should explain what is confirmed and what remains open.
  13. Dr Sen: One result is still pending, and its reviewing clinician needs to be identified explicitly.
  14. Dr Ford: Let us assign result ownership instead of assuming that whichever team sees it first will take responsibility.
  15. Dr Sen: I will include the appropriate contact for questions about the hospital changes.
  16. Dr Ford: And our team will confirm the oncology follow-up arrangements through the actual service process.
  17. Dr Sen: The patient should receive one clear explanation once the conflicting information is resolved.
  18. Dr Ford: A reconciled plan needs verified instructions, not a merged list that preserves contradictions.
  19. Dr Sen: I will send the current summary and confirm that the outstanding tasks have named owners.
  20. Dr Ford: We will provide handoff acknowledgment, keeping discharge, pending results, and treatment decisions distinct.
A pending result needs an owner

The discharge summary lists a pending result but no reviewing clinician. The two teams clarify who will review it. No result is yet available.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Hospital clinician: "The result is still ___."

Answer and explanation

C. pending

The result is not yet available in the supplied update.

2. Oncologist: "The reviewing clinician was not ___."

Answer and explanation

D. named

The summary omitted who was responsible for reviewing the result.

3. Hospital clinician: "We need explicit ___."

Answer and explanation

C. ownership

The teams must assign responsibility rather than assume another service will act.

4. Oncologist: "No conclusion can be drawn from an unavailable ___."

Answer and explanation

D. result

A pending result cannot support an interpretation before it exists.

Check the answers, then read both exchanges aloud. Swap roles on the second reading.
